## Batch 0004 as written (Chapters 81–90), Volume 02 — binding on Chapters 91 onward

**Movement 4, the forged seal. This is the volume's midpoint and it is paid. A working seaman describes a coast road that no current chart of this coast includes, the crew checks part of it on the ground with a boat and a lead, the account arrives attached to a piece of worked glass bearing a claim seal, Mara Venn proves the claim seal forged in her own competence without a diver and without a ship and cannot say who made the glass, and the two facts — the road is true and the seal is false — are carried together in one page in the yard's own book. Nothing is resolved and the volume's climax is not begun.** The chapters are the authority on what happened.

### 1. The calendar the batch actually ran on

**`outline/volume-02.md` lock L1's grid, unchanged, and the chapters ran on it without an exception.** 1 April a Saturday, therefore 2 a Sunday, 3 a **Monday**, 4 a **Tuesday**, 5 a Wednesday, 6 a Thursday, 7 a Friday, 8 a Saturday, 9 a **Sunday**, 10 a **Monday**, 11 a **Tuesday**, 12 a Wednesday, 13 a **Thursday**, 14 a Friday, 15 a **Saturday**, 16 a Sunday, 17 a **Monday**, 18 a Tuesday, 19 a Wednesday, 20 a Thursday, 21 a Friday, 22 a Saturday, 23 a Sunday, 24 a Monday, 25 a Tuesday, 26 a Wednesday, 27 a Thursday, 28 a Friday, 29 a Saturday, 30 a Sunday. **The Mondays in April are the third, tenth, seventeenth and twenty-fourth.** The batch's ten days are **Tuesday the fourth to Monday the seventeenth of April, with Sunday the twelfth and Saturday the fourteenth not used** — fourteen days, and the volume is ten chapters.

**The hearing room's slate wall was wiped Monday the third of April at eight, Monday the tenth at eight and Monday the seventeenth at eight**, and nothing was put on it on any of them, and **the batch uses the hearing room not at all.** The slate wall is named twice in the batch, in Chapter 86 on the tenth and in Chapter 90 on the seventeenth, and the clerk keeps his own page in both, and the next wipe is **Monday the twenty-fourth of April at eight**. The last sitting remains the one of **Wednesday the fifteenth of March** and there has been none since.

**A wage week falls due on a Tuesday in this batch, twice: the ninth on Tuesday the fourth of April and the tenth on Tuesday the eleventh.** **The eleventh falls on Tuesday the eighteenth of April and is not yet due at the end of the batch.** The fifth, sixth, seventh and eighth weeks were the Tuesdays of the seventh, fourteenth, twenty-first and twenty-eighth of March and remain unpaid.

**The moon, on the page once, in the fifth of April's first column: four days off new.** This follows from Chapter 74's *two days off full* on Friday the tenth of March and is the only astronomical figure in the batch.

**The *Vigil* tender's days on station are not stated in this batch and no day-count is invented.** Interpolate from the fourth of November at twenty days, as the Batch 0002 review ruled.

**The glass in the weather is a counted fact and not an occasion.** It is falling from Friday the third of April, and the batch counts **six** days on the ninth, **seven** on the tenth, **ten** on the thirteenth, **twelve** on the fifteenth and **fourteen** on the seventeenth, and it has not made up into a rain in any of them. The count is elapsed days from the third, and **a chapter that moves a day in April must move this figure with it.** **The harbor's tide bell goes once in this batch, in the road at ten past twelve on Thursday the sixth of April, and the water off the south end gives it back a hand's width of a second later — and no occasion is entered, no count is made, and Chapter 83 says on the page that there was no boat in that harbor that day with a slate on it and that the count in the yard's book stands where it stood on the thirty-first of March.** The count of logged occasions since the twenty-sixth of October therefore **stands at eleven**, and the count of occasions logged with no call in them **stands at two**, and neither is raised.

### 2. The money chain, mark by mark

**Three marks in a bag, thirty on the chandler's note and two crowns in a tin, on Monday the seventeenth of April. The tin has not been spent since the first of February and is not spent in this batch.**

| Date | In | Out | Bag | Note |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Fri 31 Mar | — | — | **5** | 27 |
| Tue 4 Apr | — | nothing moves. **The ninth week of a wage, four marks, falls due and is entered unpaid.** | **5** | 27 |
| Wed 5 Apr | **20 gross**, Iven Toft, the second seam, a herring boat's own ring and about four feet of her own warp, her own account, the yard's pump hired | the eighth 2, the share 2, air 2, rope 1, a hand 2 (Tam Slee, hired out of the *Grey Widow*), the pump 4 = 13; the yard's **7** | **12** | 27 |
| Thu 6 Apr | — | nothing moves | **12** | 27 |
| Fri 7 Apr | — | **2**, a pot of air, bought in advance against a day not yet named. **This pot is not the pot the Thursday's day runs on; it is still standing in the shed behind the pump housing at the end of the batch with the strap undone and nothing done to it** | **10** | 27 |
| Sun 9 Apr | — | nothing moves | **10** | 27 |
| Mon 10 Apr | — | nothing moves | **10** | 27 |
| Tue 11 Apr | — | **3**, a fortnight of bread, oil and candles, on the note in the creditor's own hand and out of the bag the same afternoon, **and 2 more of air, which is the second pot bought since the first of April; five in all. This second pot is the one the day's work on the thirteenth ran on.** **The tenth week of a wage falls due and is entered unpaid** | **5** | **30** |
| Thu 13 Apr | — | **2**, the day's victuals — bread, tea, and coal in a stove in a boat. **The pot of air the day ran on was the one bought on the eleventh; the pot bought on the seventh was never opened.** The *Grey Widow*'s day of a boat is Dace's and is not the yard's and is not on the page | **3** | 30 |
| Sat 15 Apr | — | nothing moves | **3** | 30 |
| Mon 17 Apr | — | nothing moves. **A shilling changes hands at the foot of the dock for the second copy of the ninth thing, is not the yard's income, and the man who pays it is a coaster's master and not Amos Pryde** | **3** | 30 |

**The full chain re-derives: 5, 5, 12, 12, 10, 10, 10, 5, 3, 3, 3** and **27, 27, 27, 27, 27, 27, 27, 30, 30, 30, 30**. The bag loses two marks over fourteen days and the note gains three, both of which are the fortnight's bread, and the yard's gross income over the batch is **seven marks out of one working day.** The two counts that matter are that **the yard's gross for fourteen days is seven marks and the bread for those fourteen days is three**, and that is the whole of what no instrument costs.

**The chandler's note, and the three counts of it, which are said out loud for the second time in three weeks and again have not moved.** The **mark figure is thirty** and it went up three on Tuesday the eleventh, and **the three counts of the weeks are the creditor's twenty-two, the yard's thirteen, and eleven as the last figure ever written in that page, and none of them has moved since the fourth of January and none of them is moved by anything in this batch's book.** The note is the biggest that page has been since the eighth of March and Chapter 87's read-out says there is nothing in the world to be glad about in that. **The note is not re-written in this batch, is not asked to be re-written, and the refusal given on the thirty-first of March is not touched.** The volume's resolution, in Movement 5, must either re-write it or say out loud that it is still not re-written.

**Debt, all standing, all unpaid, none reduced anywhere in this batch.**

- **Forty marks to Bevin Rook** — sixteen for the reading of the book from the fourteenth of October, twenty-four for the hand from the twenty-ninth — whole, not asked to be reduced, unpaid, and the boy does not ask and is not told a date and nobody in the crew asks him about it.
- **A wage at four marks a week from the first of February.** The first four weeks are paid. **The fifth, sixth, seventh and eighth fell in March and the ninth on the fourth of April and the tenth on the eleventh, and all six are unpaid, which is twenty-four marks on the seventeenth of April, in a column with no adjectives in it.** **The column holds the unpaid weeks only, and it is five lines deep on the fourth of April and six on the eleventh; it is not a count of weeks served.** The eleventh falls on the eighteenth and is entered the same way.
- **Forty marks to Mara Venn**, first after food, unpaid since the fifteenth of October, not paid, not reduced and not asked for, and **not paid in this batch.**
- **A new figure, entered in her own column and not the yard's: four marks offered in writing by a shop on the quay at Ashtide for an opinion on the seal, wanted as a document; the answer was refused on Wednesday the twelfth of April in about ninety words on her own account; the shop wrote back on Friday the fifteenth that an opinion given in a market is not a thing it can pay for.** That is the first time in five months that anybody has declined to pay her for something she did, it is a figure about her trade and not about this yard, and the forty marks are not one mark smaller for it.
- **Eight marks and a day of a boat to the harbor's bellwright**, entered under a rule on Monday the twenty-seventh of February, still not paid, and **there is no going out since the twenty-seventh of February**, so the rope on the core is a year old in January and the yard has not found the eight marks and Chapter 90 says it has not said out loud what happens in June.
- **Eight shillings and tenpence of Sella Brigg's true third of twenty marks**, undiminished, unpaid, in her own name, and the whole stack of shoal iron it came off is **still unsold, still worth twenty marks at a fair sale to a clerk's thirteen to the crew, and there is still a saw kerf in one crown shackle that nobody has accounted for.**

**Two new money facts of the batch, and they are customs rather than law.** First: **the harbor's eighth and the harbor's share are taken off a day rate on private work in April with no charter at all**, and the reason is given on the page in Nessa Pike's own words and entered in the first column with the man who asked to be named against it — *the mole, the tide gang and the wall are the harbor's, and a yard with no charter is not a yard with no customs, and a man who argued this yard out of the eighth in April would be arguing himself out of a tide gang in November.* Second: **the eighth and the share are taken off a day rate as well as off a raising, and the yard has never argued either.**

### 3. Every document and instrument created in this batch

- **The reason of Tuesday the fifth of April**, in Calder Marr's own left hand, in the yard's own book and in no office's, six lines: the second seam of the mole's own face, landward of the third seam and not within the first clause of the notice of the thirty-first; no instrument of any office used, no permit held and none asked for; nothing put in the water that is not named in the line; a herring boat's own ring and about six feet of her own warp on her own account; the party in the water proposed to be Tam Slee, hired out of the *Grey Widow* and not of the party; the limit fixed by the diver at the boat; the tender Nessa Pike and not the diver; the cutoff hers to pull without asking anybody. **Its third line, made to be read aloud twice on its own, says that there is a person in the water in this work and that he is not of this party, that the limit is his and was fixed by him at the boat before anybody was wet, that the tender is named and is not the diver, and that the cutoff is hers to pull without asking anybody; that the party is not asking this office for anything and this office is not named in the reason; and that the reason is in the yard's book because the yard keeps the practice there and will not have the two confused.** **This work DID have a person in the water. Tam Slee went over the side at eleven and was up at twenty-two, and no chapter of this batch may say that nobody went under on the fifth of April. The sentence that no person is proposed to go in the water belongs to the claim window of the seventh, which is a different instrument for a different object on a different date.**
- **The claim window of Friday the seventh of April**, in Calder Marr's own left hand, with the clerk present and unedited, read out and read into the harbor authority's ledger before anything else. **It is for one panel of worked glass of undetermined provenance, brought into this harbor on the sixth of April by Harl Dace, master, of the *Grey Widow*, of no crew of this association, and held by him and not by the party; the object is claimed by nobody, is entered in the custody room of no office, has no number in this office or the circuit office or any other, and is not one of the three objects entered in the circuit custody room on the fourteenth of October.** One date, the tenth of April; one place, this office's own ground between the sixth and ninth seams of the mole, the crown and not the toe; nothing in the water at any time; no person proposed to go in the water; no permit asked for; the party not asking the office to say what the object is; and the party would like a conservator at the examination who has no office, and the office is not asked to find her.
- **No bond.** Kest's ruling, in his own words in the ledger: a bond is pledge of the party's own, the party's own is nothing, the object is not the party's, so there is nothing to hold and nobody to be surety, **and a yard with no charter and no bond and no instrument may examine one object in daylight on one date provided it does not go in the water — which is a nicety, is the whole of the yard's right to stand on that mole that month, and was neither earned nor given.**
- **The fifth acknowledgement, entered in the reeve's own hand on the seventh of April and read back, at the asking of the party.** That the first clause of the notice of the thirty-first closes the mole's toe and inner face from the third seam to the eleventh to all work and all approach by all persons, which includes this office and this yard; **that this office has no power to lift that notice and is not lifting it and has never lifted it**; that the office is putting a party, an object and a clerk on the mole's own ground and is **treating the whole of the mole from the third to the eleventh seams as inside that first clause for the purposes of this instrument whether the crown is inside it or not, and is treating it that way on purpose, because a conservative reading of a notice is not a narrow one**; and that this is the **fifth** such entry in that office's book, the first four being a permit of the eighth of January, a reason of the twelfth, a claim window of the seventeenth and a line under a reason on the nineteenth, and **that the fifth is the only one a party asked for.** The cost of it is Kest's alone. **This is lock L24, worked for the fifth time, and the first time at the yard's asking rather than the reeve's own motion.**
- **The finding of Monday the tenth of April, in Mara Venn's own words and her own hand, at her own asking, entered in the yard's own book and in no office, and with the word *unsigned* under it and the reason — that no person in this harbor with an office has entered it and the reason why is the fourth of January.** It is carried in the two-column entry of the seventeenth and it is what is on the ninth thing on the market wall.
- **The four conditions Mara Venn states before she touches the object**, all four entered by the clerk: no diver, no water, no air and no ship; she will not describe what is in the panel and has not been in it; what she says will be about the glass and the mark, in that order; and she has no office, so it goes in a yard's book and in this office's book if the office will take it, and nowhere else, and her name goes on it in her own hand or it does not go on at all.
- **The entry of Sunday the ninth of April**: that the holder went to a house on Coombe Row at three in the afternoon and asked one question and was refused, **and that the name of the person who refused is not written at her asking, and that the boy did not write it, was not asked to, and did not ask.** It is the first line in the yard's book with a hole in it that is not a hole.
- **The entry of Tuesday the eleventh of April**: that the object is in the keeping of Harl Dace and has not at any time been in the keeping of the party, and that the party declined to hold it and gave a reason in the party's own words, entered under the line and not shortened, and that **nobody was asked to give anything up to make that happen.**
- **The ninth thing on the market wall, Saturday the fifteenth of April**, a foot and a half of writing with a nail through the top corner, in Calder Marr's own hand: the three marks and the bar in Dace's words; the check's figures, the hour, the tide and the lead; what was not checked and why; both columns with a reason against every line of the second; the conservator's finding in her words with her name at her asking and *unsigned* under it; and four lines — that **the party makes no claim to the water, to the marks, to the bar or to any use of them, and has asked for no instrument and holds none**; that **the object is in a master's keeping and never was in the party's, and the reason for declining it is on a page in a shed and not on the wall, because it is about this yard and not about the sea**; that **this sheet is not a seal, is not a claim, is not a survey, binds nobody, consents for nobody, and may be copied by any man who can read, and the party will not ask any person for anything**; and that **the count of the copies of the public sheet of the fourteenth of October is eleven and is eleven still, nothing on this wall changes that, and no holder of any of them has been asked by this yard for any of them at any time and will not be.**
- **Two copies of the ninth thing: one on the wall and one into the hands of Amos Pryde at the foot of the dock on Monday the seventeenth at the price of a shilling, which he paid and would not be given.** There is no money to print any and two is not eleven.
- **The two-column entry of Monday the seventeenth of April**, the longest in the yard's book, with four lines under the columns of which the fourth is the volume's own argument: *that the seal on the face of the object is false, and that the finding is a conservator's, in her own words and her own hand, at her own asking, and is unsigned, because no person in this harbor with an office has entered it and the reason why is the fourth of January; that the finding is about a seal and not about the sea, and that the account is about the sea and not about the seal, and that neither of the two is a reason to believe the other, and that they are in the same book in the same week because both of them are true and neither of them helps anybody; that the object is in the keeping of a man and never was in the keeping of this yard, and that the party made no claim to the water, and that this yard holds no instrument, no claim, and no paper of any kind at the hour of writing, and that this is entered as a fact and not as an opinion; that this yard does not know who held the tool, does not know who made the glass, does not know why a man wrote the name of his own boat down as the thing that owns a record of the sea, and does not intend to find out in a hurry.*

### 4. The piece of worked glass, and exactly what is and is not established about it

**It is not a custody object. It is not one of the three items. It is not in the custody room of this or any office and has no number anywhere. It is a different object in a different trade from the compartment and the three Gannet documents and it is never fused with any of them, and the claim window says so on its face.**

**What it is.** A **listening panel** — a sheet of worked glass about the size of a folded chart, four inches across the hand-hole, with an iron collar round its edge and a loop at the head of it, made to be hung in a boat's rigging and listened to from, and **not made to be entered**, and entering it would be dangerous. **Its age is NOT established and may not be asserted.** It came out of a shop on the quay at Ashtide **in the autumn**, Harl Dace paid for it, and it has been in his own keeping since; **the yard therefore knows when the shop let it go, and that is a purchase date and not a trade's figure.** Mara Venn **refuses on the page to give a figure for how old the work is** and gives the reason in her own mouth: a date is a thing you take out of a shop's book and not out of an edge, and she has not seen the book. **What she does say about the making is that the edge is dressed the way a working hand dresses an edge and not the way a man in a hurry dresses one, and that it has not been dressed that way since a bellwright she trained under was alive, and that this is a statement about care and not about time.** It was worked in a bellwright's shop on the quay at Ashtide, out of glass recovered, by a shop that says the word *willingly* the way a man says a word when he means it. **The shop is not named on any page and Mara Venn refuses to name it and refuses to name a man, and says both refusals are proper and neither is modesty.** The witness was **Corrie Ash, twenty-six, a boat hand off the *Anna Beth*, laid up in the winter with his chest, who put his hand on the panel and looked out of the shop's front window at the water and said what he saw.** Dace was in the room and heard a man describe a coast he has sailed since he was seventeen. **The thirty-year dating that an earlier draft of Chapter 86 carried, and the wear argument that went with it, are withdrawn: a witness who laid a hand on the panel while it was made cannot be a man of working age now if the glass is thirty years old, and three months of carrying in a boat cannot produce thirty years of wear. No later chapter may date the panel, and no later chapter may restore the thirty-year reading.**

**The seal.** A **claim seal**, a Registry instrument, doing the three things a claim seal does and no more: who holds the thing, where the thing came from, what a man may do with it. **Its text, read out on the mole's crown on the tenth of April: *Custodian, the sloop Grey Widow of Morrow Quay. Provenance, one panel worked at the quay of Ashtide. Limit, route use.*** It is in the office's form, in the office's order, with the office's name spelled as that office spells it, and the lettering is good.

**What is established, and it is a material and institutional finding in Mara Venn's own competence, without a diver and without a ship:**

1. **Material. The ring has nine joins in it, and a ridge of lifted glass standing on the outside of every one of them.** A Registry striking is cut once into steel by one man and the ring around the office's name is part of the same piece of steel and is one continuous curve from end to end. A ring with nine joins in it was cut in something else first — a paper, most likely, or a copper plate — and then **struck into the glass by hand.** Every one of the nine ridges runs the same way round the whole ring, which is a hand and a hammer and not a machine, and **a Registry striking has no ridge on it anywhere, because that die is pressed down and not hit.**
2. **Institutional. The custodian is a boat.** A claim seal is only true if the custodian on it can be asked things. The Registry names a person or an assembly, and either of those can be told yes or told no, can be put a limit on, and can be found again next year and asked whether it still means it said in the spring. **A boat cannot be asked anything, cannot consent to anything, cannot refuse anything, cannot be found, and cannot be asked whether it still means it said in the autumn.** So the seal is **false in the one clause that would let anybody use it** and right in every other particular a man would check.
3. **What the material half cannot do, in her own words: it cannot give a date and it cannot give a man.** *Every word of it on the outside and none of it on the inside, and that is a man's hand and a man's hammer and a man working faster than that office would have wanted the work done, and it is the whole of what a thing like that will ever give anybody.* **The wear argument — thirty years of a thumb on the outside of the ring — is withdrawn, and so is the claim that the mark and the glass are the same thirty years.**

**What is not established, and may not be settled in this volume.** **Who held the tool. Who made the glass. Why a man who wanted a permission wrote the name of his own boat down as the thing that owns a record of the sea.** That sentence is in the yard's book in the holder's own hand and on the market wall in the conservator's words, and it is the hinge of the movement. **Mara Venn says it out loud, unprompted, before anybody asks, and says: *I am not being careful. I am telling you the exact size of what my trade can do.***

**What nobody may do with it.** **No chapter of this volume, and no chapter of any volume, may read the coast road off the glass, may read any content out of the panel, or may treat the panel as a chart, a map, a network, or a key to anything.** Mara Venn states on the page, four times in one morning, that she will not describe what is in the panel, that she has not been in it, that she is not going to be in it, and that if a man asks her what a fisher saw out of a window at Ashtide she will say she has not looked and could not tell him. **The panel's content is never described in the prose of Chapters 81–90 at all.**

**Where it is at the end of the batch.** **In Harl Dace's own hands, in a sailcloth, in his own keeping, and it has never been in the yard's keeping for one hour.** It was put back on the mole steps at about five o'clock on Tuesday the eleventh of April, in front of the market, and the giving-back is entered with the hour and the reason and Dace's read-back.

### 5. What the coast road account does and does not establish

**The account is Harl Dace's, master of the *Grey Widow*, of no crew of this association, given on his own account, not paid, not engaged, and not asked to check anything first.** He is a working seaman of forty-eight who has been in this market thirty-one years, lost two of other men's on a coast further south where there is a proper chart and a light, and has never been asked for any of this by an office or a man.

**The coast.** About five miles north of this harbor's headland, **inside the water, inshore of the shoals, well inside the harbor line, and nowhere near the four hundred yards off the outer marks.** It is not the run under the town, it is not a bearing out at sea, and the yard says on the page, in Chapter 87 and again in Chapter 89, that it **claims nothing and draws no line and joins nothing.** Chapter 85 has an old woman refuse, out loud, to have her half and this coast put in the same book, and gives the reason: one is a road of water and the other is about men, and a page that holds both can be read either way.

**The three marks.** **The Old Custom House**, a cairn of ballast stones eleven feet above the rock on a low point, drying at half-tide, the top three courses black and the weed on the bottom course green; **the Nine Foot**, a sandspit with nine fathoms of a ship's stem lying on it head up, called the Accountant, white hard sand, nine fathoms over it at the middle of a spring tide; **the Kettle**, a rock the size of a good-sized boat lying on her side, drying at low water springs three days out of a month. **The bottom:** a bar of old gravel beach that comes up to two and a half fathoms in the middle of a spring. **The way in and out of weather:** the Rooks, a creek mouth a mile and a half south of the bar, the only shelter on five miles of that coast in a north-easter, entered from the south in the lee of the point **on the flood and never on the ebb.**

**The check, on Thursday the thirteenth of April, and what it cost.** Out past the headland in the *Grey Widow*, **past the headland at a quarter past ten on a fresh breeze in the north-east and a short sea outside the bar**, **with the yard's own hand lead and the yard's own slate, on no permit and under no instrument, because no office in this coast has anything to do with a bar of gravel, and with no person in the water at any time in the day.** **Six hours of flood after eleven is Iven Toft's condition for the second seam on the fifth of April and is not a condition of this day and is not spoken on it.** **Part of it, not all of it, and not the whole coast.** The cairn was checked — eleven feet by pole at half-tide, the top three courses black. The stem was checked — nine fathoms, four casts. The bar was checked — **two and three-quarters at twenty past two on the flood off a spring**, which is the figure Dace gave as his own before the boat came out, and two and a quarter on the ebb, which is the shoulder. **The lead fouled at four fathoms and a hand's width and came up a knuckle of kelp, a shoe and four feet of herring warp gone hard**, and that is somebody's loss and not the road, and it is in the second column. **The third mark was not checked**, because the Kettle dries three days out of a month and the date was not one of them, and **the date for going back is left to the tide and not to the yard.** **Eleven miles of that coast was not looked at and cannot be, no survey of anything exists, and Dace forbids the word survey out loud.**

**Cost of the check: two marks out of a bag of five**, being the day's victuals, **and a day the yard could not have sold at any price**, and nothing else, because the *Grey Widow*'s day is Dace's and is not on the page.

**What it establishes.** **The account is true of what it covered.** **What it does not establish.** **That there is a road. That the road is a road. Anything about the five miles not looked at. Anything about why there is a bar there, or a cairn, or a creek, or who put a cairn of ballast stones on a point and called it the Old Custom House. What is in the panel. Who made the glass. Why a boat is on a seal.** **It may not be explained, it may not be named as a network, and it may not be connected to anything under the town — and the two may never be fused.** The harbor authority still has no chart of what is under the town because the town was built on it, and the run's four hundred feet of ordinary figures stand as they stood on the nineteenth of January, untouched by this batch.

**Two corroborations, both free and both in the market.** **Oswin Trask**, a master of fifty-eight out of the south lay with a boat not in a lay and no paper, **says in the market in front of people that the whole of it is a tale**, and asks for the whole of it to be entered, and is entered, and is entered again as **not having been proved wrong either.** **Coll Ferrow**, thirty-one, of the second lay, boat called the *Kestrel's Sister* with a bad wheel and nine days in the harbor because of it, compares two of the figures on the wall against a slate he has carried since November and **says out loud, in front of anybody, that they are his own.** Both names are on the wall and in the book and neither man was paid and neither was asked to be right.

### 6. The dated positions of the charter, the berths, the warp, the copies, the wages and the *Sea Wren*

- **The one-season provisional working charter ended at midnight on Friday the thirty-first of March and there is no second season and no instrument of any kind in this yard's keeping.** Nothing in this batch renews it, applies for it, or names a second season. The yard's only instrument in the batch is **a claim window for one object on one date**, and it is applied for by the yard and granted by the harbor authority and is not a charter and does not lead to one.
- **The notice of the thirty-first stands whole and is not lifted, narrowed, re-served or altered.** The first clause is not narrowed: it closes the mole's toe and inner face from the third seam to the eleventh to all work and all approach by all persons. The **second** clause is the reason, on the page, that a sheet of worked glass was looked at in daylight on a mole's crown and **not indoors anywhere**, and that reason is put on the yard's own record and not in an office's, because the yard is not going to argue the reach of a notice with a woman who is not a lawyer in a shed. The **third** clause names Calder Marr, Nessa Pike and Bevin Rook for the authority's work, and **no one of them has been on the authority's work since it was served.**
- **The copies of the public sheet of the fourteenth of October: eleven, and nothing in this batch changes the count.** Two on the yard's wall, five rolled in oilcloth in the bench chest, three on the walls of crews who are not parties, one with Amos Pryde at his stall. The circuit office's type says eleven as of the sixteenth of March and the *Vigil's* slate says ten as of the eighteenth of December and both stand. **The ninth thing on the wall says the count is eleven and is eleven still, and that no holder has been asked for any of them and will not be, and the yard has asked nobody in this batch and has not delivered anything.**
- **The *Sea Wren* is at the pile under the *Vigil*** with her gear and her pump in a lighter, her master's own logbook in a naval file, and a copy of a leaf of the yard's own book in his coat. **Noll Tarner reads the ninth thing in the market on the fifteenth and says the first thing anyone has put on that wall that says what it is not for.** The crew may go ashore twice a day.
- **The berths.** The **north berth is out of service** and is the only wall in this harbor that takes a north-easter. The **ninth seam's mooring is in service and is a mooring and not a berth.** **The third lay is full.** One herring berth short.
- **Fifty fathoms of the herring fleet's winter warp lie on the north wall where ninety were to lie, and forty feet of the harbor authority's own marline is in the sea.**
- **Three tide-gang men of the harbor authority are off again from the first of April**, the tide gang being three men short in April as it was in February, and Neale was on the second seam's face on the fifth with two others.
- **The mole-head light is not on and will not be.** It went on at dusk on the eighteenth of March and was taken in on the twentieth and has not been lit since, because a light on the mole head costs oil and the repair account has been empty since the middle of February.
- **The hard at Ashtide is unanswered and has been since the ninth of November.** The *Kittiwake* is on the hard with her bilge blocked in and no cradle under her, and the harbor's carpenters are free and have not been to her.
- **The pump.** The yard's own pump is the only gear in this harbor a man can hire that is not the harbor authority's, and it **went out once in this batch, on the fifth of April, at four marks, hired and not lent, and has stood idle since**, and Chapter 88 names the consequence in a line: it had not been out of the shed in eight days when the yard went out to check a bar of gravel and could not sell the day.
- **The harbor's crane, its barge, its pump and its hand crane are the harbor's, on the authority's own account, and the yard's book does not carry another office's money twice.** No harbor money enters this batch's chain.

### 7. The bodies and the crew

- **Calder Marr**, 32, line diver with an unstable tide-ear capacity used twice in Volume 01 and not used since. **No dive of his own in this batch and no entry of any kind into anything.** **No power stage of any kind is taken and none may be.** What changes in this batch is method and a refusal: he asks a reeve for a claim window and lets the reeve enter a breach in his own hand at his asking, and he gives up the only claim the yard has ever had to a real object, in a shed, in front of the man who brought it, and says the reason in his own mouth. **The condition is named once in this volume, in Chapter 88, on a working man's deck, for the sixth time since the fourteenth of October** — the ringing in both ears, the second sound under the first arriving late, the inability to tell reliably which sound is in the boat — and Nessa Pike's sentence is said to the wheel and not to him. **It may not be named again in this volume.**
- **Nessa Pike**, 27, overrules him and wins again, and wins three times in the batch: the two answers to two men on one morning are hers by argument, the eighth and the share are taken in her trade's terms and not the law's, and **she refuses to make a cutoff out of a man swinging a lead and refuses to witness a conservator's finding because a party does not witness**, and she argues the giving-back of the glass on the yard's own ground. Her last word in the batch is the most expensive: **the yard that owes a boy four marks a week and a bellwright eight marks will not put a man off a boat in its shed**, and she says it in a road and says she will say it again if anybody asks her.
- **Bevin Rook**, 22, the wrist nine weeks old on the fifth of April and taking four hours of a slate and a stone to make it ache, the sling off, the hand that does not close, and a bonesetter's book that says a boat hand in June and a shipwright who has told him to be off the stone by the middle of May whatever his own arithmetic says. He swings a hand lead four times on the Thursday and gets better each time, he puts the fouling in the second column with a reason, he does not write a name that an old woman asked him not to write and was not asked to write and did not ask, and he says the thing nobody asked for twice — on the eleventh, that a wall with a nail in it is not a seal, and on the seventeenth, that he has counted nine read-backs on the page and was told to count none of them. **Nobody in the crew asks him about the concealment and nobody did.**
- **Harl Dace**, master of the *Grey Widow*, 48, of no crew of this association, a working seaman and not a clerk and not a reeve and not a conservator and not a mystic. He asks for the slate and the book and the two columns and pays nothing, wants to be written down whether he is right or wrong, refuses the word *survey*, forbids the yard from choosing its own tide for the third mark, says the boat is better on a lead than the boy and is not put on a lead, and gives the second copy of the ninth thing to a man who is not him. **He is a witness, not a party, and the yard never asked a question of him and never took a shilling from him.**
- **Mara Venn**, 30, no standing, a trade. She states four conditions before she touches the object, she proves the seal forged in her own competence, **she says the hinge sentence before anybody asks it**, **she refuses the asking for a figure for the third time in five months and the reason is new and the principle is not** — *twice you wanted a number for a wall; this time you want a number that would let a man in a city write that a conservator of Morrow Quay certifies, and I will not be certified by a party* — she puts the word *unsigned* under her own finding and says why, she is refused four marks in writing by a shop for an opinion and enters the refusal and the figure in her own column, and she says out loud on the mole that the forty marks are not one mark smaller for it. **She does not break with the Registry in this batch or in this volume, and her break is Volume 4.**
- **Ness Tarrant** — **Neave Tarrant, of the third lay, nine winters, out since the twenty-eighth of February, not found, and NOT opened in this batch.** One line in Chapter 90: there is not one line about him in the yard's book, the boy entered on the twenty-first of March that he had looked twice, nothing has changed on that page since, **nobody in this yard has been asked anything about him by anybody, and the yard has not gone.**
- **Sera Mott**, 68, retired fisher. **Asked once in this volume, in a private scene, on Sunday the ninth of April, and she refuses.** She holds to the December sentence that she will say no one more time in about two years, **her name is not written in the yard's book at her asking, and the entry says she was asked and refused and the boy did not write the name and was not asked to and did not ask.** She gives two men who have been out on that coast inside ten years for nothing, and she refuses to have her half and the coast road in one book and gives the reason.
- **Noll Tarner**, of the *Sea Wren*, reads the ninth thing and says the first thing on that wall that says what it is not for, and answers his own month-old question: a record of a dock is for the people who come after you.
- **Iven Toft** of the *Mercy Ledge*, second lay, fifty, refused on the fourth and served on the fifth; **the bad thumb is the unnamed master of no crew of this association who wanted the mole's toe, and not Toft's, and only one of the two men in this batch has it.** **Oswin Trask**, of no lay, fifty-eight, objects in a market. **Coll Ferrow**, second lay, thirty-one, confirms in a market. **Corrie Ash**, of the *Anna Beth*, the witness in the panel, named on Dace's mouth and not examined. **Neale** and two of his gang were on the second seam's face on the fifth. **The second copy of the ninth thing went to a coaster's master at the foot of the dock on Monday the seventeenth at a shilling, which he paid and would not be given for nothing — and not to Amos Pryde, who is a holder of a copy of the sheet of the fourteenth of October and not of this one, and who is named on the wall on the fifteenth and is offered nothing.**
- **Ors Kest**, 44. He grants a claim window, he rules that there is no bond, **he enters the fifth acknowledgement in his own hand and says the cost of it is his, and says it is the first one a party asked for**, he tells the yard in advance what the circuit office will do with a competent false seal, and he says the yard's protection is never having had the object. **He is not a friend and he is not to be softened, and he spends nobody else's winter in this batch — the volume's resolution keeps that for Movement 5.**
- **Alden Marr** is at the far bench in Chapters 81, 82 and 90, is not asked for anything, did not read the two columns, and Calder Marr did not go over to him. **No fragment, no report talk, no second story, and the batch says so in its own words on the seventeenth.**

### 8. The concealment, the delayed note, and what the batch did not do

**The concealment is hidden and it is not touched.** The Oren mark is in no record, in no office, in no book and not in Kest's chart notation. **The one false line on the unverified slate is still false and the slate was not moved**, and Chapter 90 says so and says why in the same paragraph as it has said it in March. **The boy with the book has not asked and has not been asked, and on the ninth of April he did not write a name he was asked not to write and was not asked about and did not ask.** **The woman with no office who knows what is in the anchor has still never been asked to say it**, and in this batch she is the one who refuses twice, out loud, on her own ground, and the holder does not ask her and does not argue her refusals and is told by a shipwright that he has asked four people nothing this month. **A concealment may be told once, to one person, in a private scene, at a price. It was told once, in Chapter 38. It is not told again here and the volume closes with it hidden.**

**The delayed note: eleven logged occasions since the twenty-sixth of October, unchanged, and the count of occasions logged with no call in them is two, unchanged, and neither is raised.** **The harbor's tide bell goes once in the batch and no occasion is entered and no count is made and Chapter 83 says on the page that the count stands where it stood on the thirty-first of March.** **The bell is not an instrument and does not sound in this batch at all.** The core is on the harbor's ground in eight and a half feet off the south end with its waist fouled and its tongue half worn through; **the going out costs eight marks and a day of a boat, the eight marks are owing, and there has not been a going out since the twenty-seventh of February**, and the rope on it was bought on the eleventh of January and a rope is a year, and Chapter 90 ends the batch on the fact that nobody in that yard has said out loud what happens in June. **The answer inside the note is not approached, not named and not resolved, and remains reserved to the Volume 15 midpoint.**

**What this batch may not be used for, and did not use.** **The compartment is not referred to and the three-step finding is not restated.** The claim window says on its face that the object **is not one of the three objects entered in the circuit custody room on the fourteenth of October.** The dressed aperture and the fouling that gives no hour are not referred to. **The nine-line mark is not explained, and the Nine Harbors, the Quietening and the Crown Seal are not named.** **No custody object is asked for and none returns, and the yard does not ask for the custody room; the municipal claim on the third item stands and is not withdrawn, and the yard will not make a request.** **The reeve's ruling that no line may be drawn between the three points stands and is not lifted, and nothing in this batch draws one; the withdrawal is still on a wall and the difference is still not stated as a measurement anywhere.** **The run under the town is not touched.** The **first echo's witness** stays where he is. **Ilya Serr, Halven Rook and Captain Veyra Holt appear nowhere in this batch, on paper or otherwise** — and Holt's only appearance in Volume 02 remains the signature on the paper of the eighth of November already nailed to the market wall. **No new antagonist, no new institution, no new final enemy, and no senior examiner in a room.** The *anonymous letter and surviving crew list* remain unused. **The volume's climax is not begun: no fishing crew is trapped, there is no glass storm, and no equipment is destroyed.**

### 9. The costs this volume may not waive again

- **Three marks in a bag.** Fourteen days, one working day, seven marks gross, and the note at thirty because of bread.
- **Twenty-four marks of unpaid wage on the seventeenth of April**, in a column with no adjectives, and the eleventh week falls on the eighteenth.
- **Forty marks to the boy and forty to the conservator and eight marks to the harbor's bellwright and eight shillings and tenpence to Sella Brigg**, all standing, all unpaid, and the bellwright's eight not before October.
- **A refused job at the mole's toe and a market that has worked out that this yard only works when a man cannot go to the office.**
- **A pump that can be hired and cannot be sold, and an instrument that does not exist and a customs that do.**
- **A claim given up on purpose**: the only claim the yard has ever had to a real object, given back in a market, in front of the man who brought it.
- **A finding that is true and that no person with an office in this harbor may enter or sign**, and a conservator refused four marks in writing for an opinion she gave in a market.
- **A wall with a nail in it and two copies**, and eleven copies of another sheet, and four holders who have all refused and none of whom has been asked.
- **A man of the third lay who is not back**, and a line in a book saying there is nothing in it, and a yard that has not gone.
- **A bell nobody has looked at since the twenty-seventh of February**, on the town's own ground, with a rope a year old and a tongue half worn through, and nobody in that yard has said out loud what happens in June.

### 10. Proposed canon changes

**None.** No bible file, no outline, no ending and no lock is amended by this batch. **Three additions and one discipline, and each is a discipline rather than a rule.**

1. **The coast road, as a described and partly checked physical fact, and the worked glass, as an object with a proved forged seal.** **This is the Volume 2 midpoint and it is paid, and the finding is a proposition about records and not an answer**: *a record can be a lie about provenance and a true account of a road, and the two facts have to be carried together.* **A later volume may not resolve it, may not explain the road, may not name it as a network, may not connect it to anything under the town, and may not read any content out of the panel.** What a later volume **may** do is carry the proposition — that a record's truth about one thing says nothing about its truth about another, and that a false seal is worse held by a party than held by nobody, and that a document no office will sign can be the only true document in a harbor.
2. **The harbor authority's fifth acknowledgement, and the first one a party asked for.** Lock L24 is worked a fifth time, in a new form — a claim window, with no bond, and a conservative reading of a notice the office will not narrow — and **the cost of it is still Kest's alone and it is still never free.** No later chapter of this volume may work ground the notice closes without it.
3. **The eighth and the share, taken in April with no charter, on a trade argument and not a legal one.** This is a practice of this yard and of this harbor and it is on the page in the first column with the man who argued it named against it. It binds the yard's later arithmetic; it does not change any instrument, and it is not an instrument.
4. **A discipline, not a canon change: the counts do not move with the calendar.** The three counts of the chandler's note stand at twenty-two, thirteen and eleven as last written, exactly as in Movement 3; the count of the copies of the public sheet stands at eleven; the count of logged occasions in the delayed note stands at eleven and the two empty answers stand at two. **No later volume may advance any of them by arithmetic.**

## Batch 0004 review repairs — the disposition of every finding

A reviewer pass over Chapters 81–90, the four state files, `outline/volume-02.md` and the queued `batch-0005/PROMPT.md` returned **FIX**, with five required repairs and ten prose, precision and file-reference repairs. The reviewer's own log is not kept in this repository, and `state/current.md` § *Batch 0004 review repairs* holds the short form. **No chapter was restarted, no scene was cut, no cost was dropped, and no planned plot was altered.** Movement 4, the coast road, the panel, the claim window, the fifth acknowledgement, the check on the water, the ninth thing on the wall and the volume's midpoint argument are all as `outline/volume-02.md` has them, and `outline/ending.md`, `outline/series.md`, all six bible files and all twenty-four locks are untouched.

**The calendar was checked first, because the fifth required repair was a calendar in the queued prompt and it would have been drafted from.** The anchor is **1 April a Saturday**, given by `outline/volume-02.md` lock L1 and confirmed by Chapter 90's Monday wipe on the seventeenth. Therefore 18 April a **Tuesday**, 24 April a **Monday**, 25 April a **Tuesday**, 30 April a **Sunday**, and the Mondays in April are the third, tenth, seventeenth and twenty-fourth. **The chapters were already correct on every one of these and the state files agree with them.**

1. **Chapter 82's own record contradicted the dive staged on the same page.** Its third line, read aloud twice, said that *no person is proposed to go under at any time in this work, that there is therefore no air limit upon any person*, and the first column six clauses later said *no person in the water* — while Tam Slee, a hired diver, went over the side at eleven and came up at twenty-two with a shackle and nine feet of warp, a diver-set limit and a pullable cutoff. **The dive stays and the two false sentences go**, for three reasons that are the volume's own: Chapter 88's refusal to invent a cutoff for a man on a lead is a refusal that only means something *because* the fifth of April had a real one; hard rule 4 requires a named tender, a fixed limit and a cutoff for a person in the water; and the sentence that no person is proposed to go in the water belongs to the claim window of the seventh, which is a different instrument, a different object and a different date. **The third line now says what is true of the fifth — that there is a person in the water and he is not of this party, that the limit is his and was fixed by him at the boat before anybody was wet, that the tender is named and is not the diver, and that the cutoff is hers to pull without asking anybody** — and it still ends on the practice clause the rest of this volume rests on, that the reason is in the yard's own book because the yard keeps the practice there and will not have the two confused. **The first column now reads *no person of this party in the water at any time, the diver being of another boat***, which is checkable from the mole and is the fact a reader needs. **§ 3 above is corrected to the same, and it now says in terms that no chapter may say nobody went under on the fifth of April.**
2. **The worked panel could not be both thirty years old and bought out of a shop in the autumn.** Chapter 86 had Mara Venn date the work to thirty years *because that is how that edge is dressed*, and then argue thirty years of a thumb on the outside of the ring, and conclude that the mark and the glass are the same thirty years; **Chapter 83 has Dace buying the panel at a shop at Ashtide in the autumn, Corrie Ash of working age laying a hand on it while it was made, and Dace carrying it in a dry locker, a wheelhouse and a coat ever since.** **The dating and the wear argument are withdrawn and the material finding is rebuilt on something that does not depend on age.** The nine joins stand, and the second half of that finding is now the **ridge: a ridge of lifted glass standing on the outside of every one of the nine joins, and every one of the nine running the same way round the ring, which is a hand and a hammer and not a machine, where a Registry striking is pressed down and not hit and has no ridge anywhere.** That is a stronger check than the wear was, because it can be made on a thing that was made last month. **Mara Venn now refuses on the page to give a figure for how old the work is** and gives the reason in her own mouth — *a date is a thing you take out of a shop's book and not out of an edge, and I have not seen the book* — and what she offers about the making is that the edge is dressed the way a working hand dresses an edge, *and that this is a statement about care and not about time*. **This sharpens the midpoint rather than weakening it: the yard knows when the shop let the panel go, and that is a purchase date and not a trade's figure, so the man who bought it and the woman who examined it know two different things and neither knows a maker.** The institutional finding — *a boat cannot be asked anything* — is untouched, the hinge sentence is untouched, the two columns are untouched, and the panel's content is still never described. **Chapter 83's *cloudy with age* is now *cloudy*, and Dace's *I have had three months to think about that* is now *all winter*, which is what the autumn supports.**
3. **The queued `batch-0005/PROMPT.md` carried a calendar shifted four days.** It gave 18 April as a Saturday and 30 April as a Thursday, which makes the twenty-fourth a Friday and contradicts the Mondays it states two lines later, Chapter 90's Monday wipe on the seventeenth, the wage weeks on the eighteenth and the twenty-fifth, and the slate wall's next wipe. **The grid in that prompt is now the grid this section states, its *last day of April is a Tuesday* appears twice and is now *Sunday the thirtieth*, and its hard rule 4 misdated the hand-lead precedent to the seventh of April** — the seventh is the claim window, and the refusal to invent a cutoff for a man swinging a lead is **Thursday the thirteenth**, in Chapter 88, in the boat. **Hard rule 4 now also carries the fifth of April as its other half**, so the next batch inherits *a person in the water and a person on a lead are two different kinds of day and the yard keeps them apart* instead of inheriting a contradiction.
4. **Air was bought twice for one day and the state file half-saw it.** Two marks of air on Friday the seventh, two more on Tuesday the eleventh, and Chapter 88 saying the pot had been filled on the Friday, with § 2 above booking both and then writing *the air was already bought on the seventh*. **The two purchases are now two pots and the bag reconciles without a duplicate in it.** The seventh's is a pot bought in advance against a day not yet named, and **it is still standing in the shed behind the pump housing at the end of the batch with the strap undone and nothing done to it** — which is two marks a yard with three in its bag cannot waste, and is therefore a cost the batch now carries instead of a hole in it. **The eleventh's is the second pot, and it is the one the Thursday's day ran on**, bought the same afternoon the check was agreed. Chapter 87 names both pots on the page, Chapter 88 says which pot the day used, and the table in § 2 above carries it.
5. **The second copy of the ninth thing had three holders and three prices across four files.** Chapter 89 gave it to Amos Pryde, Chapter 90 gave it to a coaster's master at a shilling, and § 2 and § 7 above said Amos Pryde paid a shilling. **The coaster's master and the shilling stand, and Chapter 89 is corrected to them**, because Chapter 90's coaster's master is the same man who came up the hill at half past five that evening asking for a room for a man off a boat, and that echo is load-bearing for the batch's pull into Movement 5. **Chapter 89 now says the copy is not going to Mr Pryde's stall, and gives the reason in a market and calls it not unkind** — *he has been named on a wall of mine since September and he will want this and he will want to have been asked for it, and asking him is asking him* — and names the shilling and why there is one: a thing that is free is a thing that is owed, and the yard is not going to pretend it gave a man something. **Pryde keeps his copy of the sheet of the fourteenth of October and no holder of anything is asked for anything**, which is the batch's own argument and is now true of the one man it names.

**Prose, precision and file-reference repairs.**

6. **Chapter 83's broken sentence, and two facts inside it.** *Harl Dace, on the second of March, at a fish dock, in front of witnesses, and put it in your book…* had no main verb, named a day that is neither the fourth nor the sixth, and called Bevin Rook's injury *a boy's broken finger* — **in this volume the injury is a crushed hand and a wrist of the twelfth of January, and a broken finger is Wend Crale's.** It is now **I am Harl Dace of the *Grey Widow* and I said it on the sixth of April at a fish dock in front of witnesses, and you will put it in your book because it is true**, and the answer a man in four years will get is **a boy's hand that will not close**, which is the phrase this book uses for it everywhere else.
7. **Chapter 88 gave the boy a forger of his own technique and a longer career than he has.** He watched *Tam Slee swing it four times in March* — Slee worked the second seam on the fifth of **April**, and on the Wednesday the boy was on a stone with the slate and did not watch swings at all — and he watched *a man do it for twenty-six years before that*, **and Bevin Rook is twenty-two and has been in the yard five months.** It is now **a man who had been in that water forty years swinging over a fall into a flood running green over the stone, and a boy who had stood on a stone with a line in his hand since he was eleven.** The seventh swing is still better than the sixth.
8. **The *in about N words* figure was wrong twice and the wrong figure had been copied into the state files.** Chapter 85 said Nessa Pike said it in *about nine words* and she said forty-four; Chapter 87 said Mara Venn said it in *about eleven words* and she said a hundred and twenty. **The device is the house's and is used accurately in Chapters 61, 72 and 89, so it was not removed: Chapter 85's figure is now forty, and Chapter 87's is now the observation it was reaching for — that she said it in one go without stopping anywhere, which she had not done in that shed in five months.** The eleven is corrected in `state/chapter-summaries.md`.
9. **The unpaid-wage column was given a depth it cannot have.** Chapter 81 said *nine lines deep* against five unpaid weeks and Chapter 87 said *ten* against six. **The column holds the unpaid weeks and not the weeks served, so it is five lines deep on the fourth and six on the eleventh**, § 2 above now says so in terms, and the queued prompt carries the same two figures. Chapter 90 was already right in switching to *a column with no adjectives in it* with no depth, and that phrasing is untouched.
10. **Two men had the bad thumb.** Chapter 81's unnamed master of no crew of this association who wanted the mole's toe is *about sixty, with a bad thumb*; Chapter 82 gave the same thumb to Iven Toft, who is fifty. **In a book that identifies people by their hands one of the two loses it, and Toft loses it** — he is given Chapter 81's own description back instead, *the back of a neck that a north-easter had worked on for thirty years*, which is how Chapter 81 already introduces him. **The mole-toe man keeps the thumb**, and § 7 above now says which of the two has it.
11. **Chapter 87 broke the batch's own counted series.** *A wind outside that had not made up into anything for five days*, on the eleventh, when the glass has been falling since Friday the third and the batch counts six on the ninth, seven on the tenth and ten on the thirteenth. **It is eight days**, which is the eleventh on the batch's own rule of elapsed days from the third. **§ 1 above no longer credits the batch with a count on the fifth that no chapter gives**, and it now states the rule and that a chapter which moves a day in April must move the figure with it.
12. **Chapter 90 read the sixth of April in a future tense on the seventeenth.** *There has been nothing since the fifth and there will be nothing on the sixth* is now **there was nothing on the sixth and there has been nothing since the fifth.** The same paragraph's **Gross in the last ten days, twenty, on the fifth of April** put a day twelve days outside the window, and the entry it belongs to covers the whole span: **both are now the last fourteen days, and every mark of the twenty is on the fifth**, which is what § 2 above has always said. The figure did not move; the window did.
13. **Chapter 88 mixed units in a sentence meant to be plain.** *The difference between those two things is four miles and eleven fathoms* is now **four miles of water and a gravel bar that a man can be aground on in a flat calm** — which is Dace's own account of the bar and the reason a man who does not know where can be on it for six hours with the tide over his mast.
14. **`state/current.md` cited a review log that does not exist.** Lines 13 and 14 both pointed at `logs/batch-0003.review.log`; `logs/` holds only `close.log` and `models.log`, and no `logs/batch-000N.review.log` has ever existed in this repository for any batch. **The reference is corrected to the authority that does exist — `state/continuity.md` § *Batch 0003 review repairs*, where every Batch 0003 finding is disposed of — and no file was invented to satisfy a citation.** The same false citation was standing in two more places in the state files, in the Batch 0003 heading of this file and in `state/open-threads.md` § *Volume 02, Batch 0003*, and **both are corrected on the same principle.** One reference is left standing and is deliberately so: `reviews/volume-01/batch-0003.md` cites the same path for the **Volume 01** batch, and `reviews/` is not a file this phase may edit. **Extended, and the description here corrected, by the Volume 02 close audit of 26 September 2026: this ruling was true in its correction and wrong in its account of the directory — `logs/` has never held a batch review log, for any batch, in either volume. Fifteen further citations naming Batches 0001, 0002, 0004 and 0005 stood in the four state files and are withdrawn on this same principle. See § *The reviewer log family* in this file.**
15. **§ 5 above attributed the fifth's condition to the thirteenth.** *Six hours of flood after eleven* is Iven Toft's condition for the second seam on the fifth of April; **the *Grey Widow* was out past the headland at a quarter past ten on the thirteenth and no such phrase is spoken on it.** § 5 now says so in terms.

**One unflagged defect of the same family was found while applying the rest and is repaired here.** Chapter 90's money read-out gave the gross of the whole span as *the last ten days* in two places, while § 2 above, `state/current.md` and the queued prompt all call the span fourteen days and the yard's gross for it **seven marks out of one working day**. **Both are now the last fourteen days**, and no figure moved.

**Verified in the repair and not changed.** The money chain reconciles mark by mark (**5, 5, 12, 12, 10, 10, 10, 5, 3, 3, 3** in the bag; **27, 27, 27, 27, 27, 27, 27, 30, 30, 30, 30** on the note) and the tin holds two crowns untouched since the first of February. The three counts of the note's weeks are still twenty-two, thirteen and eleven and none of them moved. The eleven logged occasions and the two empty answers are untouched and the bell still goes once in the batch with nothing entered. The false line on the unverified slate is still false and the slate was not moved. The reeve's ruling that no line may be drawn between the three points stands. Alden Marr confesses nothing and Calder Marr does not go. The concealment is hidden and was told once, in Chapter 38. No custody object returns. The compartment is not referred to and the three-step finding is not restated. The nine-line mark is not explained. Ilya Serr and Halven Rook appear nowhere and Holt only as a signature on a paper already on a wall. Lock L24's fifth acknowledgement stands in the reeve's own hand, at the yard's asking, with the cost his alone. The road is not explained, not named as a network, not connected to anything under the town, and the panel's content is never described. **The volume's midpoint is intact and its turn is intact: the seal is false and the witness is real, and neither of the two is a reason to believe the other.** The climax is not begun. No interface vocabulary, no panel, no stat block and no second person. No duplicated paragraph and no exact repeated sentence anywhere in the batch.
