## Batch 0005 as written (Chapters 91–100), Volume 02 — binding on the volume audit and on Volume 03

**Movement 5, the rescue and the writ. THIS CLOSES VOLUME 02.** A wharfinger and a fire and a warping, a boy mending trousers and a hat in a market, a glass storm, a claim window and a pot of air three weeks standing, a man in five fathoms of water off the inner shoals and the equipment that went with him, thirty marks in a shed from four people who said the cause out loud first, a provisional writ with a reporting clause and four names on it, a bell's second column and a lead plug in a chest, a going out in a flat calm and six copies in six hands, and a wall. **The volume's central pressure is resolved: the crew has a writ, and it is a longer leash with a name on it, and the act that earned it cost a hand lead, a hose, a claim window and two days' work, and both facts are true and neither cancels the other.** The chapters are the authority on what happened.

### 1. The calendar the batch actually ran on

**`outline/volume-02.md` lock L1's grid, unchanged, and the chapters ran on it without an exception.** 1 April a Saturday; therefore 2 a Sunday, 3 a **Monday**, 4 a Tuesday, 5 a Wednesday, 6 a Thursday, 7 a Friday, 8 a Saturday, 9 a **Sunday**, 10 a **Monday**, 11 a Tuesday, 12 a Wednesday, 13 a Thursday, 14 a Friday, 15 a Saturday, 16 a Sunday, 17 a **Monday**, 18 a Tuesday, 19 a Wednesday, 20 a Thursday, 21 a Friday, 22 a Saturday, 23 a Sunday, 24 a **Monday**, 25 a Tuesday, 26 a Wednesday, 27 a Thursday, 28 a Friday, 29 a Saturday, 30 a Sunday, and **1 May a Monday**. **The Mondays in April are the third, tenth, seventeenth and twenty-fourth, and the first of May is the fifth.**

**The batch's ten days of story are the eighteenth, twenty-fourth, twenty-fifth, twenty-sixth, twenty-seventh, twenty-eighth and twenty-ninth of April and the first, second and third of May**, and the chapters carry their chapter-length sequences across them: **Chapters 91, 93 and 94 each cover two dates, and Chapters 97 and 98 are both the first of May and are two rooms on one day.** The last sitting remains the one of **Wednesday the fifteenth of March**. **The hearing room is used twice in this batch and neither use is a sitting: the clerk read the yard's reason into the office's own ledger in the hearing room over the tide-gang store at half past nine on Thursday the twenty-seventh of April, with the slate wall bare because it is wiped on Mondays, and the one sitting was on Monday the first of May at ten. The market wall is used once, on Wednesday the third of May, and neither hearing-room day is the market day.**

**The slate wall is wiped every Monday at eight whether there is a sitting or not, and the only wipe named in this batch is Monday the first of May at eight — there is none on the twenty-fourth of April. CORRECTED BY THE VOLUME 02 AUDIT: the first draft of this section placed a wipe on the twenty-fourth and put the chalk figure on that day, and no chapter does. On Monday the first of May Kest wrote on it for the first time since the fifteenth of March, in chalk, in a hand trained to look neutral, and the wall had stood bare for six weeks; the rule is that a figure on that board is a figure for a week and a figure in the yard's book is a figure for good, so the next wipe is Monday the eighth of May, which follows from the rule and is not a quotation.** The clerk said what a box can hold five times in the batch and said on the twenty-ninth of April that he will keep his page whether the wall is up or not.

**A wage week falls due on a Tuesday in this batch, twice: the eleventh on Tuesday the eighteenth of April and the twelfth on Tuesday the twenty-fifth.** The fifth through the eighth fell on the Tuesdays of the seventh, fourteenth, twenty-first and twenty-eighth of March and remain unpaid. The tenth fell on the eleventh of April and is also unpaid.

**The glass in the weather is a counted fact and not an occasion.** It is falling from Friday the third of April, and the batch counts **twenty-one** on the twenty-fourth, **twenty-three** on the twenty-fifth, **twenty-four** on the twenty-sixth and **twenty-eight** on the first of May, and it had not made up into a rain until about eleven o'clock on Monday the first of May, when it stopped falling and did not rise, and it stood still on the third. **A chapter that moves a day in this window must move this figure with it.** **The wind in the batch is a north-north-east from about four o'clock on Sunday the twenty-sixth, lasting nineteen hours, and it comes round into the south-west on Monday the first of May about two o'clock in the afternoon, and goes into the south.**

**The *Vigil* tender's days on station are not stated in this batch and no day-count is invented.** Interpolate from the fourth of November at twenty days, as the Batch 0002 review ruled.

### 2. The money chain, mark by mark

**Thirteen marks in a bag, twenty-three on the chandler's note, and nothing in a tin, on Wednesday the third of May. The tin held two crowns from the first of February until the twenty-seventh of April and is empty.**

| Date | In | Out | Bag | Note | Tin |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Mon 17 Apr | — | — | **3** | 30 | 2 crowns |
| Tue 18 Apr | — | nothing moves. **The eleventh week of a wage, four marks, falls due and is entered unpaid.** The column is seven lines deep, twenty-eight marks, the fifth of March the oldest | **3** | 30 | 2 crowns |
| Thu 20 Apr | — | **3**, a fortnight of bread, oil and candles, in the creditor's own hand, on the note and out of the bag the same afternoon, and the three is the biggest single item on that page since the eighth of March | **0** | **33** | 2 crowns |
| Mon 24 Apr | — | nothing moves. The fire, the warp, the hose. **The twelfth week falls due on the twenty-fifth, not on this day** | **0** | 33 | 2 crowns |
| Tue 25 Apr | **8**, the second lay's hose, in one tray and one hand: eight shillings taken at a fish market, being one shilling, two shillings, a shilling between two women, three more shillings and four lots of threepence, which is a shilling, and **Edric Pringle's four marks on the top of it**. **The twelfth week falls due and is entered unpaid; the column is eight lines deep, thirty-two marks, the deepest it has been** | — | **8** | 33 | 2 crowns |
| Wed 26 Apr | — | **1**, a lamp, oil and a hand of coal for a stove | **7** | 33 | 2 crowns |
| Thu 27 Apr | — | **1**, rope and twine at a sailmaker's. **The tin is opened for the first time since the first of February and pays one shilling and sixpence, the harbor authority's fee for a writ, in advance and not returned whether the writ is granted or not** | **7** | 33 | **8s 6d** |
| Fri 28 Apr | **6**, the claim window's day rate at the yard's own rate — the air at two marks and the pump at four — paid by the harbor authority on the office's own account, counted out of the clerk's coat in front of three people, the hour written and read back | **the tin's last eight shillings and sixpence**, the day's victuals and a stove and coal in a boat. **The tin is empty** | **13** | 33 | **empty** |
| Sat 29 Apr | **30**, from four people in the shed at their own rate, each saying the cause first: Voke 12, Dace 8, Pringle 6, and 4 out of a hat carried by Coll Ferrow | the bellwright **8**, the boy **16**, the note **6** = 30 | **13** | **27** | empty |
| Sun 30 Apr | **4** | **4 straight onto the note in the creditor's own hand and never into the bag, because the creditor was in the market with a cart** | **13** | **23** | empty |
| Mon 1 May | — | nothing moves | **13** | 23 | empty |
| Tue 2 May | — | nothing moves | **13** | 23 | empty |
| Wed 3 May | — | nothing moves. **A shilling changes hands at the foot of the dock for a printed slip, is not the yard's income, and is not in the bag** | **13** | 23 | empty |

**The full chain re-derives: 3, 3, 0, 0, 8, 7, 7, 13, 13, 13, 13, 13, 13** in the bag and **30, 30, 33, 33, 33, 33, 33, 33, 27, 23** on the note, and the tin **2 crowns, 8s 6d, empty.** **The bag stands at nothing from the afternoon of the twentieth of April to the evening of the twenty-fifth, five days, and the yard lives on a creditor's page through all of them; a fortnight of bread on the twentieth is what empties it.** And **of the sixteen marks that went into the boy's wage on the twenty-ninth, every mark came out of four hands in a market and not one out of the yard's own work, and the six that went onto the note came out of the same four hands.** **The only money this harbor has paid that yard since the first of February is the six marks of the claim window's day rate, and the yard says so on the page in the clerk's hearing.**

**The shoal iron is NOT sold in this batch and the yard is offered nineteen marks for the stack and a shilling for the crown shackle with the saw kerf in it, separately, and takes neither.** The reason is entered: **a yard that has declared a thing about its own gear in a book cannot then sell that gear without a buyer reading the declaration first, and that is what the declaration is for and it was not put there to make the iron worth more.** The stack stays on the yard's own ground, unsold, worth twenty marks at a fair sale to a clerk's thirteen, with the kerf. **Sella Brigg's eight shillings and tenpence is NOT paid in this batch** — it is the smallest figure in the book and the one that has waited the longest, and **on the twenty-ninth the yard took thirty marks in a shed and every mark of it was gone before the day was out, eight of the thirty to a man who keeps a bell, sixteen of the thirty to a boy, and six of the thirty onto the creditor's page, and the eleven pence is the smallest figure in the book and it is the one that has waited the longest, and the yard did not touch it and will not.**

**Debt, all standing, all unpaid, none reduced, except the one that is paid.**

- **PAID — eight marks and a day of a boat to the harbor's bellwright, Corvin Sarn**, on **Saturday the twenty-ninth of April**, out of four hands in a market and **not one mark of it out of the yard's own work**, and the yard entered that he was not paid on Friday the twenty-eighth and is not asking to be, and that the going out of the second of May is what the eight marks bought. **This is the first money that has gone out of this yard's book to anybody since the first of February.** He refused to have it in a wage column, and would not be thanked.
- **Sixteen marks of a boy's wage out of somebody else's pocket**, being the ninth, tenth, eleventh and twelfth weeks of April, four lines, **and the fifth of March, which is the oldest line in the column, is deliberately left in it because he is not going to take a man backwards in his own book.** The column closes the batch **four lines deep at sixteen marks**, which is the same figure and the same depth as on the thirty-first of March, and the yard says that too. **No mark of the sixteen came out of the yard's own work and the boy says so in his own mouth on the page.**
- **Forty marks to Bevin Rook** — sixteen for the reading of the book from the fourteenth of October and twenty-four for the hand from the twenty-ninth — **stand whole, unasked to be reduced, unpaid, and not one mark of them paid in this batch.**
- **A wage at four marks a week from the first of February**, the first four weeks paid, the fifth through the eighth (March) unpaid and still in the column, **and the ninth, tenth, eleventh and twelfth all paid on the twenty-ninth of April out of the market.** **Four weeks fell due in April** — the ninth on Tuesday the fourth, the tenth on Tuesday the eleventh, the eleventh on Tuesday the eighteenth and the twelfth on Tuesday the twenty-fifth — and none of the first four of the twelve is paid.
- **Forty marks to Mara Venn**, first after food, unpaid since the fifteenth of October, **not paid and not paid out of the market money, and the writ's reporting clause does not reach her and cannot.** She says in the hearing room on the first of May that she is not asking for them and is not going to be thanked for not asking, because not asking is not a gift to anybody, and holds to the twenty-second of March: if a mark of it is offered she will walk it down the dock and put it on the wet stones.
- **Eight shillings and tenpence to Sella Brigg**, undiminished, unpaid since the sixth of February, and now unpaid **and** with about four hundred feet of her own warp at the bottom of the harbor since the twenty-fourth of April, and the fire having been free.

**One money figure is the harbor authority's own, and it is one shilling and sixpence.** The writ's fee is in the office's own schedule of charges, was made by somebody who is dead, is not returnable, and is the only money into that office's account in this harbor since the middle of February, and the clerk said so out loud on the steps of the shed at eight o'clock in the morning on the twenty-seventh of April.

**What the air cost and what it bought.** No pot of air is bought in this batch. **The pot bought on Friday the seventh of April, standing unopened in the shed behind the pump housing with the strap undone, is opened on the morning of the twenty-eighth and is the air the rescue ran on, and it is the whole of the air in this harbor.** Nessa Pike insists that its age be read out on a mole before anybody is wet, in the yard's own words, and the word *once* is entered, and two men in the water read it. The pot is empty on the Friday night and there is no air in this harbor and none to be had.

### 3. Every document and instrument created in this batch

- **The reason of Friday the twenty-eighth of April**, in Calder Marr's own left hand, read into the harbor authority's book at half past nine on Thursday the twenty-seventh **in the hearing room over the tide-gang store, which is the office's own record room and is not a sitting, with the slate wall bare as it has stood since the fifteenth of March, it being wiped every Monday at eight whatever is or is not on it**, **carrying the sixth of the reeve's own acknowledgements** — that the first clause of the notice of the thirty-first closes the mole's toe and inner face from the third seam to the eleventh, which includes this office and this yard, that this office has no power to lift that notice and is not lifting it and has never lifted it, that it is treating the whole of the mole from the third to the eleventh as inside the first clause whether the toe is inside it or not **because a conservative reading of a notice is not a narrow one**, that this is the sixth such entry and the first five were on a permit of the eighth of January, a reason of the twelfth, a claim window of the seventeenth, a line under a reason on the nineteenth and a claim window of the seventh of April, and **that this one was asked for again by a party, which is the second time, and that the cost of it is his and always was and is never free.** The reason also names **two divers not of the party, Tam Slee and Wil Slee, each fixing his own limit at the boat**, the tender as Neale of the harbor's own tide gang and not a diver, the cutoff his to pull without asking anybody, **nothing under the bedded material**, the air with its purchase date and its three weeks stated, **and the party's own sentence that the party is not on this office's work and is standing on that ground as the owner of hired gear and not as a workman, and that if the office requires a different reading the office's reeve will say so now and the party will stand somewhere else.**
- **Kest's entry of the same morning**, in his own hand, read back: that **the yard is having the best of it**; that in January, in February and on the seventh of April the yard put a hand lead on ground he had closed and had to write a line in his own book about it, and that on Friday the twenty-eighth the yard puts its air on the same ground for a fee and the reeve signs the order that pays it; that this is not a favour and not a breach; and that the yard should be able to say in four years that it asked for a job and was given a bill, at its own rate and not the office's, and the whole of it in a book and not in a conversation — **and Calder Marr makes him write that in the ledger as well, and Kest writes it in his own hand and says he will be annoyed about it for a fortnight.**
- **The claim window of Friday the twenty-eighth of April**, for one date, the reach and the old ground tackle lying on the mole's face between the third and fourth seams, **nothing under the bedded material**, the party not on the authority's work. **It is worked and logged and raised nothing, and it is gone, and a man in a city is going to read that and call it a window that was missed, and no instrument in this coast holds the difference.**
- **The salvage account of Friday the twenty-eighth, read out on a fender in twenty seconds** with the pot hissing: ground, purpose, the diver (Calder Marr, of the party), the tender (Neale, of this harbor's tide gang, not the diver, with a line and not a boat of his own), the limit of twenty-two minutes fixed by the diver at the boat, the cutoff the tender's to pull without asking anybody, the air out of a pot bought on the seventh of April and standing three weeks, **and the sentence that the eighth of that pot is going on a hand lead in the diver's own hand and the rest of it is on two men in a boat.**
- **The fire party's order of the twenty-fourth of April**, on the harbor authority's own motion and its own order, recorded in Kest's own words in the hearing record of the first of May, **together with the cause of the warp in the words of the woman who lost it, said first by her in a market on the twenty-fifth and entered in no office's book until the twenty-ninth, and together with the north wall figure of the twenty-eighth** — fifty fathoms of the herring fleet's winter warp on it where ninety were to lie, and the money that would replace the other forty having gone on four sets of moorings and a season of warp in the fortnight before the first of March, on the authority's own account, with no fathom bought since, **entered as a fact about the office and not as a favour and not as an apology.**
- **The two-column read-back of Friday the twenty-eighth** with the first column, the second column, and **three lines under them, in Calder Marr's own hand**, because there is no column for a thing that is both a fault and a life: that the ninth seam's mooring is ground the first clause closes, that the notice has not been lifted and the yard has not asked for it to be lifted and will not, **and that a man without an instrument and without a permit worked on that mooring on his own account for six marks, and was not lawful there, and is alive**; that the notice did not save him and the yard will not say in any book that it did, and will not say that the notice is not worth the paper it is on, and both of those are in one page and neither is a reason to touch the closure; and that the yard put a gland and a pot of air and a hand lead's line into five fathoms to get a man out of ground this office's own notice closes and does not intend to write that sentence twice.
- **Harl Dace's entry of the twenty-ninth, in his own words and in his own right hand, read back by him and not by the boy**: that a claim window worked and logged and raised nothing has no paper at the end of it in this harbor and he has been here thirty-two years and has never seen one; and that **the paper is what a man reads in a city and in this harbor it is what a man with a bad hand put his name against at seven in the morning.** It is the first thing anybody in this harbor has said to the yard about a writ, and it goes up the coast.
- **The market entries of the twenty-fifth and the twenty-ninth**, four causes said first in four mouths and entered in the first column with the man and the hour against each, and **the four marks out of a hat in the second column with a reason against it, read out loud in the shed in front of four people**: a man in a hat is not a man anybody can ask again in four years, he is not on a crew's list or in a lay or on this office's page or the harbor authority's page, and no office in this coast can be asked about him by name, and the only paper that will ever exist about that four marks is the boy's.
- **The non-use of the yard's own rule, entered in the first column with Nessa Pike's name against it:** the rule is air, then rope, then food, then the harbor's eighth, then the harbor's share, off the top of anything raised, in that order, every time; **it was not used on the twenty-ninth of April because this is not a raising, a hat, a woman's warp, a man's boat and a herring skipper's day rate are not the same thing and are not the same men, and I am not going to take a fifth off a fire party and call it the harbor's share.**
- **The second column of the bell, read back on Monday the first of May at four o'clock with the writ lying on the bench**, four men reading it and one of them the holder, and **the third column of the going out, created on Tuesday the second of May, for what the boy said** — he is not in the boat, the four men cannot check him, the top of the column carries the time it was read on the steps and the time it was written in the book, **and the difference between the two is the whole of what is being fixed and the boy is not going to pretend the two times are the same time.** It is the writ's doing, and it is entered in both books and it is of the yard's book and not of any office's.
- **The entry of the lead plug**: four descriptions by four men, four of which do not agree, all four entered, the reason against the whole of it being the shortest line in the book — **that a thing in this yard's book that this yard has not got is worth more to it than the same thing in a drawer, and that the party did not find it and cannot describe it and will not be asked, and that the bellwright is keeping it and the bellwright is not of this party.** The plug is in the chest, and the boy has written on the lid that there is nothing in it that anybody in this harbor can check, **and a number in it is not written down anywhere and was not written on a slate and the bellwright refused to say what it is.**
- **The provisional writ of Monday the first of May**, granted by the harbor authority, **which is a new instrument and not a renewal of anything.** It runs to the first of March next; it is inside the harbor line and bounded by the outer marks; **the four-hundred-yard bar off the outer marks stands and is not narrowed by it**; the ground is the mole from the first seam to the twelfth, the dump, the north wall, the first and second seams, all ground landward of the third seam, and private ground within the line; the harbor's eighth and share follow the holder on anything raised; **it is not the Second Bell license and does not lead to one**; it may be suspended after an inspection for cause by one man with a key and a book; **and the four names on it are Calder Marr, Nessa Pike, Bevin Rook, and Tam Slee of the *Grey Widow*, hired and not of this association, who was asked in person on the afternoon of the first of May, in a shed, and who said he was content to have it, in his own words, and was not asked twice.** Its last line: **that this office is not the office that can renew it, that the second season is not this office's to give, that nothing in the instrument says there is a second season, and that if there is one it will arrive out of a city from a man who has never been inside this harbor, and the holder will be told about it when it arrives and not before.**
- **The reporting clause, attached, read aloud twice and entered unsummarized.** Every finding of worked glass, whole or in part, and every raising of material from ground which any other party claims, within four days, with a copy to the harbor authority, stating the place, the hour, the person in whose keeping, and the limits; and where a finding is disputed between persons the harbor authority **may seat one of them and one other and take such finding as it is given, without prejudice to any claim**; and the holder shall produce the writ on demand to any officer of the circuit office or of the harbor authority. **It carries three names on paper and no face in the room: requested in writing by the magistrate of the circuit office of this coast, signed by the master of a cutter holding a claim in this harbor, sealed by Magister Ilya Serr, senior Registry examiner.** It is on the market wall under the naval paper of the eighteenth of March by the third of May, read.
- **The yard's objection to the clause, unsummarized, in two hands, up the coast with the writ in a sealed jacket on the second of May**: that the clause is capable of being read to reach a bar of gravel and a man's account of a coast, and the holder has made no claim to the water and holds no instrument in respect of it, and if any office wishes to read it that way the holder will say so in writing on the day the office asks and not before; that the clause asks for findings and a finding is not a conclusion, and the holder reported two findings in public in April, one against its own interest and one the work of a person with no office and no fee, and will go on reporting findings and will not go on reporting conclusions; and that **the holder does not object and takes the writ with it, because a yard with no paper cannot work, and a yard with a reporting clause knows what it has agreed to.**
- **Mara Venn's own paper, going up the coast on the second of May.** Not the yard's, not a copy of anything of the yard's, three clauses about a false seal and a boat, her name at the foot in her own hand, **three questions about a chart, which she does not state to anybody in the hearing room and will not state in a market in June.** **A Volume 03 chapter inherits this document and may use it, and she remains a trade and not an office and is not the yard's.**
- **The tenth thing on the market wall, Wednesday the third of May**, in Calder Marr's own hand, under the naval paper of the eighteenth of March: eleven lines at the head, the ninth thing entire, the rescue's two columns, the bell's second column, the head of the whole thing again at the bottom, **and a list of six hands and a mouth.**
- **Six hand-copied copies, in six hands, of six boats, none of the yard's**, carried out on Tuesday the second of May: **Harl Dace to Elias Voke and Sella Brigg, Edric Pringle to Coll Ferrow and Oswin Trask, in her own hands, on the bus.** The head says that no copy is in this yard's keeping, that one is on the market wall and one is in the harbor authority's ledger in the clerk's own hand, and that the party asks no person to do anything with it. **Oswin Trask's copy is correct in every figure and carries one line the wall does not have, in his own hand: *and I have not been to it since the fifteenth of April and I am not going and that is mine and not yours.***
- **The count of the copies of the public sheet of the fourteenth of October: eleven, unmoved.** A slip went up under the tenth thing with the eleven names and the four holders the yard cannot reach named, and a shilling changed hands for it at the foot of the dock from a coaster's master and not from Amos Pryde.

### 4. The bodies and the crew

- **Calder Marr**, 32, line diver with an unstable tide-ear capacity used twice in Volume 01 and not used since. **He does not enter anything in this batch and no power stage of any kind is taken and none may be.** He is not barred from the water by anything; the notice's third clause bars him from the **authority's** work, and the rescue is not the authority's work. **He is in five fathoms of water for eleven minutes and comes up against the tender's decision, and a small cold thing arrives in the leg of his right arm in the shape of the burn that comes back hours after a rope has come off a man's hand, and he acts on it before he can say it.** **The condition is NOT named in this batch** — it is named on the twenty-sixth of April only as the fact that he could not place a sound, asked for a sentence twice, was given it twice and could not say which of the two he got, and the boy enters that in the second column with a reason against it, and nobody in the crew asks him about it. **It was named for the last time in Chapter 88 and may not be named again in this volume.**
- **Nessa Pike**, 27, overrules him and wins, and the wins are the chapter's beats: she refuses to put one of the yard's men in the town's fire; she insists the pot's age be read out on a mole and enters the word *once*; she refuses to use the yard's own money rule on a fire party and signs her name against the non-use; she reads the second column of a bell and takes the whole of it in one page; she walks a man of fifty-one down a hill and not to talk; **and in the hearing room she refuses the yard's habit of doing to the circuit office what the circuit office did to the yard and says so out loud.** She has overruled him six times in this batch.
- **Bevin Rook**, 22, **the hand that has been going since the fourth of April and is not written about in this batch until Chapter 100, where the yard says it out loud in a hearing room in front of a clerk of the circuit office and says the boy is not to be sent because his hand is going.** He swings nothing, he is in the water once in a dinghy, he gives a fire party a number and not a name, he mends the third pair of trousers six times, he reads the writ back, he writes the third column's head with two times in it, he enters that he has been to the fishery twice since the twenty-first of March and has had his hand open the whole of both times **and has not put that down because nobody in this yard has asked him and he is not going to start, and he calls it a second omission and enters both in the same breath so that nobody can come along in four years and say he hid one and not the other** — and nobody in that crew asks him one word about it, then or afterwards. **He is to be off the stone on the fifteenth of May whatever his own arithmetic says, and the bonesetter's book still says a boat hand in June.**
- **Harl Dace**, 48, of no crew of this association, a witness and not a party, never paid, never asked to be right. He commits the *Grey Widow* to a going out, carries two copies, says the sentence about a piece of paper that goes into a hearing record, and **says the only sentence he says that night: "That is not my boat."** His man of fifty-one with the bad wheel cannot place a sound either and is not on a lead and is never told why.
- **Corvin Sarn**, 50, the harbor's own bellwright of Morrow Quay, **PAID**, and the craft is the closing chapter of the volume. He names the mechanism and the price, refuses to have the money in a wage column, refuses to make a repair or a finding out of a thing nobody has asked him about, **refuses to have the number in the lead plug written on a slate, and then refuses to take the plug out of the room on purpose**, and says on the way out the door that a bell in the dark is a bell that is not there. **He teaches nobody and no stage is taken and none may be; the Bellwright stage is Volume 5 and requires a crew.**
- **Mara Venn**, 30, no standing, a trade. **She is expensive and right and she does not break.** She says four times in a hearing room, before anybody asks, that the writ cannot reach her and that she is not of it; she holds to the twenty-second of March's sentence about the forty marks; and **she sends a paper of her own up the coast on her own account with her own name on it, and does not tell the yard what it says, and says she is not an assistant and is not going to be one.** Her forty marks are not paid and the market money did not touch them. **Her break is Volume 4 and it is not near.**
- **Elias Voke**, herring skipper of the *Anna Ray*, 51, nineteen winters disputed, **names the price of a night's work at a day's rate in his own words and will not pay for a bell and asks a clerk to check that the February warp and the Friday warp are not the same fault**, and says out loud that the writ is a longer leash and that he would have given a good deal to be wrong about the second season.
- **Edric Pringle**, 54, master of the *Anna Beth*, of the second lay, **difficult about money and says the cause out loud first, twice**, is paid four marks for four hundred feet of her own warp, is read the tenth thing on a market wall as its fifth reader, and says a yard has put the cause of her loss right way up for the first time in her life and is not going to thank them for it in a market with forty people in it. **Her eight shillings and tenpence is still unpaid and her warp is on the bottom.**
- **Harl Dace's man of fifty-one with a bad wheel of his own**, unnamed, a man of the *Grey Widow* who cannot place a sound, is never put on a lead and is never told why, and is walked down a hill and not to talk. **His bad wheel is his own and it is not the *Kestrel's Sister*'s, and the two men are not the same man and no chapter may make them the same man: Coll Ferrow, thirty-one, of the second lay, is the man of the *Kestrel's Sister*, and the bad wheel that has kept that boat in this harbor for nine days is the boat's.**
- **Iven Toft, Neale, Wil Slee, Tam Slee, Rowlandike, Petch, Wil Slee's brother, Sella Brigg, Coll Ferrow, Oswin Trask, Ewan Tristram, Ambrose Sell, Ivo Trethewey, Neave Tarrant, Amos Pryde, Harl Dace, Corrie Ash, the copying clerk of the Morrow circuit office** are all in this batch in ordinary working ways. **Ambrose Sell is a clerk of the circuit office and the mouth of the reporting clause and is not an antagonist and must not become one.**
- **Neale**, foreman of the harbor's tide gang, **is the named tender on the claim window and on the rescue, is not a diver, pulls the cutoff at eleven minutes, does not apologize and does not look away, and is on the mole for the first time in this yard's book on the strength of a yard's reason read into a municipal ledger.** His own line: *I have read a tide-gang notice in my life and I have never once been the name on it. Thank you for writing me down before you wrote yourselves down.*
- **Alden Marr** is at the far bench in Chapters 91, 92, 93, 95, 98 and 100, is not asked for anything, and **the bench moves under the drip on the twenty-sixth of April and the two of them are in one room for nine hours and do not speak. Calder Marr does not go over to him, and the volume closes with that not-going as the relationship.** No fragment, no report talk, no second story. **No confession.**
- **Tobias Wren** is in the shed and is not asked anything and is not injured.

### 5. The dated positions of the charter, the berths, the warp, the copies, the wages and the bell

- **The one-season provisional working charter ended at midnight on Friday the thirty-first of March and there is no second season and no renewal of anything in this harbor.** **The writ of the first of May is not a charter, is not a renewal, and does not lead to one**, and its last line says the second season is not the harbor authority's to give and that whatever comes will arrive out of a city from a man who has never been inside this harbor. **A Volume 03 chapter inherits a named absence and a four-day reporting clock that has started.**
- **The notice of the thirty-first stands whole and is not lifted, narrowed, re-served or altered.** The **second** clause is on the page twice as a reason and is not argued by anybody in this yard: the worked glass was looked at on a mole's crown and not indoors, and a lump of green glass that came up off the mole's toe on the twenty-eighth went into a bucket and was not looked at. The **third** clause names Calder Marr, Nessa Pike and Bevin Rook for the authority's work and **none of the three has been on the authority's work since it was served**, which is why the tender on the claim window is Neale and not Nessa, and why the yard is on the mole at all only as the owner of hired gear. **The harbor authority has now entered the fact six times in its own book and the sixth was on the twenty-seventh of April and was the second one a party asked for.**
- **The copies of the public sheet of the fourteenth of October: eleven, unmoved.** A slip went up on the third of May with the eleven names and the four unreached holders named and the count stated, and a shilling changed hands for it. **No holder has been asked for any of them by this yard and will not be, in this volume or any volume.**
- **The berths and the wall.** The **north berth is out of service** and is the only wall that takes a north-easter, and it took a sea on the night of the twenty-eighth with fifty fathoms of the herring fleet's winter warp on it where ninety were to lie, and forty feet of the harbor authority's own marline is still in the sea. **The ninth seam's mooring is out of service from the first of May with no end of it entered**, it is a mooring and not a berth, the stone is not to be taken up and the fouled shackle is not to be freed, and the ground line between the third and the ninth seams is untouched and is not to be cleared by anybody under the writ or any other paper Kest has ever signed. **The third lay is full.** **A second-lay boat, the *Kestrel's Sister*, with a bad wheel in her, is in the basin and has been for nine days at the start of the batch, and the man of her is Coll Ferrow, thirty-one, who is of the second lay and whose figures have been on a wall since the fifteenth of April and who is one of the six hands.**
- **The fire party, the chain and the cart.** The harbor authority's fire party of the second lay has a handbell, a hose cart and about a cart's worth of water in a cistern, **because the cistern is charged on a rota on a Monday and a Thursday by a man who walks out to the sluice, and the man did not walk out on Monday because the office cannot pay a man to walk out to a sluice.** The party's wet hose was laid out in eleven lengths on the second lay's boards on the twenty-sixth and is unusable for the rest of the season. **A chain and the hose cart stood in front of the last lay's wharf for the evening of the twenty-fourth and a tide-gang man, Petch, is stood off the wall from the first of May to the tenth to mind them, and the mole steps will not be cleared in June.**
- **The third lay's boat was against the second lay's wharf from the eleventh of April to the evening of the twenty-eighth with forty men in a bucket chain, and could not move, and her chain was found on the ninth seam's mooring pole, which is not a bollard, and the fire party went out in her at about a quarter to ten on the Friday night and got her chain onto a bollard before midnight.**
- **The mole-head light is not on and will not be**, the repair account has been empty since the middle of February, **and Sarn names the consequence in a shed in front of four men: a bell in the dark is a bell that is not there, and a third lay that went dark on the nineteenth of March is going dark again.**
- **Three tide-gang men off since the first of April, four from the first of May.** **The harbor authority has no boat of its own after the evening of the twenty-eighth until the barge is returned, and it committed that boat knowingly and said so in advance out loud.** **The yard's own compressor's gland, about ten feet of its own delivery hose and its hand lead's line are gone and cannot be replaced in this harbor, and the yard has no air.**
- **The hard at Ashtide is unanswered and has been since the ninth of November.** The *Kittiwake* is on the hard with no cradle and the harbor's carpenters are free and have not been to her since the twenty-eighth of November. **Nineteen winters of dues are disputed and unpaid.** **The fouling on the ground line between the third and the ninth seams is untouched for the fifth recorded time.**
- **The *Sea Wren* is at the pile** under the *Vigil*, with her gear and her pump in a lighter, her master's logbook in a naval file and a copy of a leaf of the yard's own book in his coat, and the crew may go ashore twice a day, and Noll Tarner reads the tenth thing on the wall on the third of May and is refused the word *network* and produces a better answer.

### 6. What the coast road has become, and what it still may not be used for

**The road is published, in six hands, in six boats, and half-believed, and it is not resolved.** The tenth thing on the market wall carries the ninth thing entire — three marks, a bar, a check of the cairn at eleven feet and the stem at nine fathoms and the bar at two and three-quarters at twenty past two on the flood off a spring, both columns with a reason against every line of the second, the conservator's finding unsigned, and the two men who have spoken about it, one of whom called it a tale and has not been proved wrong either. The sixth of the eleven lines at the head is **a question about charts and it does not explain anything**: the bar is on this harbor's own chart as a blank, a blank is not a wrong figure and cannot be argued with, there is nothing inshore of the headland for five miles north on any chart made by any office on this coast, and a man of forty-eight has worked it for thirty-one years and is a witness and not a party.

**The yard refuses the word in public.** Noll Tarner says *network* on a market wall on the third of May and is told no, and the reason is on the page: **the day that word is on a wall in the yard's hand is the day every man in that market has a thing to be frightened of**, and then Tarner supplies the better form himself, and it goes in his own mouth in the first column because a man in a market can be asked again tomorrow — **the question is who edits the charts, and it is a question about a man with a pen, and about forty offices on this coast draw water and every one of them draws what it has sounded, and the day a man did want to moor on five miles of a shore, somebody is going to find out what his office has got.** The word *network* is used once, by a rival, and refused.

**A Volume 03 or later chapter inherits: the road is described, partly checked, published, doubted and half-believed, and it is not resolved. It may not be explained, may not be named as a network, may not be connected to anything under the town, and the two may never be fused. Orison may be spoken of only as a place seventy fathoms down, and the yard says on the market wall on the third of May that it cannot put the seventy fathoms and the eleven feet in the same sentence and will not. The nine final routes are named in the bible and none of them may be named in the prose.**

### 7. The delayed note, and what the bell did and did not do

**The count of logged occasions since the twenty-sixth of October stands at eleven and is not raised. The count of occasions logged with no call in them stands at two and is not raised.** No occasion is entered in this batch. **The harbor's tide bell goes at about two o'clock on the morning of the twenty-eighth of April and again at about two o'clock on the afternoon of the second of May, and on the second of May the delay out of the water off the south end is a hand's width of a second, and the boy takes the figure for the delay himself at about two o'clock on Tuesday the second of May on a flat calm and reads it back twice — the same morning, in the same hour, on the same flat calm, by the man on the mole's steps with the slate and the stopwatch — and the third column of the book says on the page that no occasion is entered, that the count stands at eleven where it stood on the thirty-first of March, and that a figure taken on purpose on a flat calm with a slate and a stopwatch is not a note heard out of the weather, and that a boy who has counted eleven of them is not going to make it twelve by writing a number.** The delay **did not move** in three months and a half and nobody in this harbor can say why, and Sarn says from a ladder that he will not stand in a boat and tell them why it has not moved. **No chapter may place that figure on the first of May, which is the day the writ was granted and the day the second column of a bell was read.**

**A glass storm came on the twenty-sixth of April and it is a physical and psychological hazard and not a monster, and it is written in the second column with a reason against it and nobody may name a cause for it.** What it did: a boy of twenty-two on a roof heard his own voice about a yard behind him in a yard where nobody had said anything; a man of fifty-one with a bad wheel of his own was told twice, flat and out loud, to come his wheel round, while he had it in both hands; and a diver in five fathoms of water heard the *Grey Widow*'s own call out of the port quarter of his own head, three fathoms above the water, unhurried and not out loud, and **the delay on it is not given and the yard does not intend to invent one.** What it is: a local pressure event in which sound, light and perspective reorder around overloaded glass, and it came through the water and not off it, and it is in all of it.

**The bell did not become an instrument.** It is fouled, it has a frame cut through the casting on one side, it has two parts of a chain in a lead plug that is a piece of lead about eleven inches under a bell at eight and a half feet in a stone mole in a working harbor with a **number in it that is not written down anywhere**, and a new rope bought on the eleventh of January. **The going out is a person, in a boat, in the weather; the price is eight marks and a day of that boat; it is the same in any water; and it recurs in about four years, and the yard has told the four of them now so that there is no argument about it in four years.** **The Bellwright stage is Volume 5 and is not taken here.**

### 8. The costs this volume closes with, and the costs it may not waive again

**Paid by the resolution, in the order the outline requires:**
1. **The eight marks to the harbor's bellwright — PAID**, out of four hands in a market and not out of the yard's own work.
2. **Sixteen marks of a boy's wage arrears out of somebody else's pocket** — out of the market in whole, four lines, four hands, and not one mark of it out of the yard's own work, and the yard says how much of it is.
3. **The yard's own debt to Mara Venn — NOT PAID**, and not paid out of the market money, and not reachable by the writ.
4. **A Kest payment that cost somebody who is not the yard — PAID**: about four hundred feet of Edric Pringle's own warp off her own wharf on the twenty-fourth of April, the ninth seam's mooring out of service from the first of May with no end of it entered, and a tide-gang man stood off to mind a cart.
5. **The chandler's note — NOT RE-WRITTEN, said out loud, on a market wall, on the third of May**, with the three counts of its weeks unmoved since the fourth of January, and the yard's reason from the thirty-first of March standing unreversed.
6. **A provisional writ with a reporting clause, taken anyway, with the objection in the record unsummarized, and a longer leash with a name on it.**

**Not waived:**
- **No air in this harbor, no compressor gland, no delivery hose, and a hand lead's line being made badly by one man at night in a net loft.**
- **No charter, no second season, no renewal, and a four-day reporting clock that has started against a yard that has been wrong in public twice this month.**
- **A claim window worked, logged and raised nothing, and no instrument in this coast that holds the difference between a worked window and a missed one.**
- **A notice of the thirty-first that is not lifted, that a man was inside when he nearly drowned, and that the yard will not ask to have lifted and will not argue.**
- **A bag that stood at nothing for five days and a tin that is empty, and a page at thirty-three for bread.**
- **The fifth of March, the oldest line in a wage column, left in on purpose by a boy of twenty-two who will not take a man backwards in his own book.**
- **Eight shillings and tenpence to a woman, unpaid, and four hundred feet of her warp on the bottom of the harbor.**
- **A seal with a boat's name on it, in a master's keeping, that the yard gave back on purpose in a market, and whose forged finding no person with an office in this harbor may enter or sign, and which the yard has published unsigned and will not have certified.**
- **A lead plug in a chest with a number in it that is in no book, and a plate that was dug in a shaft on the mole's toe in February and is not in this batch and is not referred to in this batch.**
- **Neave Tarrant, of the third lay, nine winters, out since the twenty-eighth of February, not found, and the book has one line about him and the yard has not gone and asked nobody anything.**
- **A man of fifty-one with a bad wheel who cannot place a sound and is never put on a lead and is never told why.**
- **A wharfinger who stood in a doorway for six hours because the yard had nothing to do anything with.**
- **Twenty marks of unsold iron, a saw kerf, and a declaration in a book that a buyer will read before he buys.**

### 9. Proposed canon changes

**None.** No bible file, no outline, no ending and no lock is amended by this batch. **Four additions, and each is a discipline rather than a rule.**
1. **The provisional writ of salvage working is a new municipal instrument and is defined on the page.** It is issued by the harbor authority, runs to a date, names its persons, names its ground, carries the eighth and the share, may be suspended after an inspection for cause, is not the Second Bell license and does not lead to one, **and is not a charter and does not renew one.** Its reporting clause is a Crown attachment and not part of the municipal instrument. **A later volume may not widen the writ's ground past what it names, may not treat it as a charter, and may not have any office other than the harbor authority renew it.**
2. **The yard's practice gains a third column, for what the boy said when he was not in the boat, with the time it was read and the time it was written in the book both entered.** It is created by the writ's four-day reporting clause and by a boy's hand that is going, and it is the yard's own and is in no office's book. **This is a method, not a rule, and no later volume may retro-apply it to a date before the second of May.**
3. **The Kest acknowledgement is worked a sixth time and is entered in the ledger, on the reeve's own motion, over a claim window of one date and two hired divers and a harbor-authority tender.** The cost is still the reeve's alone.
4. **A discipline, not a canon change: the counts do not move with the calendar.** The three counts of the chandler's note stand at twenty-two, thirteen and eleven as last written. The count of the copies of the public sheet stands at eleven. The count of logged occasions in the delayed note stands at eleven and the two empty answers stand at two, **and a figure taken on purpose on a flat calm is entered as a figure and not as an occasion.** No later volume may advance any of them by arithmetic.

**Two clauses of `outline/volume-02.md` are discharged in a different form than the outline describes, and the volume audit must rule on both. They are recorded here rather than left for the audit to find, because a divergence in the volume's two central clauses is not a canon change and may not be hidden behind one.**

- **The climax.** The outline asks for a rescue of a **trapped fishing crew during a glass storm** that **destroys the equipment needed to claim a legal salvage award**. The chapters gave **one man, not a crew**; a glass storm on the **twenty-sixth of April** and a rescue in an **easing north-northeast on the twenty-eighth, three days later**; and **no award ever claimable, because nothing was raised.** The equipment destroyed is the yard's own — a compressor's gland, about ten feet of its own delivery hose and its hand lead's line — and **the paid official route was not abandoned but interrupted**: the claim window was worked and logged, raised nothing, and is gone, and a claim window is an office instrument on ground this office's own notice closes, **and the yard was never permitted there as a workman at all.** Nothing in this volume is waived and nothing in the outline's clause is contradicted in its facts; the clause is discharged in another shape, and the shape is the volume's.
- **The resolution.** The outline asks that the coast road become **evidence of a concealed network** on a public wall. The chapters **refuse the word in public** and put a question about a man with a pen on the wall instead. The six copies in six hands, the wall that names no holder, the count of eleven and the four unreached holders are all as the outline asks. The word is not used by the yard and is used once by a rival and refused, and **the road is not resolved, is not explained, and is not connected to anything under the town.**

**The concealment is closed hidden, as the volume requires.** The Oren mark is in no record, in no office, in no book and not in Kest's chart notation; **the one false line on the unverified slate is still false and the slate was not moved**; the boy with the book has not asked and has not been asked, and has now entered that he has been to the fishery twice and had his hand open both times and has not put it down, calling it a second omission and entering both omissions in the same breath; and **the woman with no standing left knows what is in the anchor and has still never been asked to say it, and in this batch she is the one who says, unprompted and first, that the writ cannot reach her and that she is not of it.** A concealment may be told once, to one person, in a private scene, at a price. It was told once, in Chapter 38. It is not told again here.

---

## Batch 0005 review repairs — the disposition of every finding

A reviewer pass over Chapters 91–100, the four state files and the queued `workspace/volume-02/close/PROMPT.md` returned **FIX**, with thirteen required repairs and a list of prose, precision and mechanical items, **and three further weekday errors and one broken vessel-name were found and repaired while the repair was being applied.** **The review was the reviewer agent's log, cited at the time as `logs/batch-0005.review.log`; that file is not in this repository, and the Volume 02 volume audit has withdrawn the citation. This section is the authority for every finding below.** **No chapter was restarted, no scene was cut, no cost was dropped, and no planned plot was altered.** Movement 5, the fire, the glass storm, the claim window, the rescue, the man found, the writ, the reporting clause, the bell's second column, the lead plug, the six hands and the wall are all as `outline/volume-02.md` has them, and `outline/ending.md`, `outline/series.md`, all six bible files and all twenty-four locks are untouched.

**The calendar was checked first, because every date in this batch had to be measured against the same grid and a state file that is a day out will hand the volume audit a calendar it cannot use.** The anchor is **1 April a Saturday**, given by `outline/volume-02.md` lock L1 and confirmed three times over in the batch: Chapter 90's Monday wipe on the seventeenth, Chapter 91's Monday wipe on the twenty-fourth, and the slate wall wiped on the Monday the first of May with the writ sitting behind it. Therefore 18 April a **Tuesday**, 20 April a Thursday, 22 April a **Saturday**, 24 April a **Monday**, 25 April a **Tuesday**, 26 April a **Wednesday**, 27 April a Thursday, 28 April a **Friday**, 29 April a **Saturday**, 30 April a **Sunday**, 1 May a **Monday**, 2 May a **Tuesday**, 3 May a **Wednesday**. **The chapters were right on this grid in substance and wrong in a dozen places by name, and the state files were wrong by name from the twenty-fourth onward.** Every repair below is against this grid.

### The thirteen required repairs

1. **The money chain did not close, in four places, and one of them was a whole day of income.** Chapter 92 booked *eight marks in a bag* out of a tray of eight shillings and Pringle's four marks; **the claim window's day rate — the air at the yard's own two marks and the pump at the yard's own four, which Chapter 94 says the harbor authority paid on the office's own account — never reached the bag at all**; Chapter 96's thirty in and thirty out left the bag where it was with twenty-five marks unaccounted; and § 2 above booked 25 April **+8** and 29 April **30 in / 30 out** and then asserted a bag *empty from the twentieth of April to the thirtieth, seventeen days*, which two chapters contradict and which is eleven days. **The chain is now re-derived from the chapters and every figure in it is the figure on the page.** The twenty-fifth is **eight marks in one tray and one hand** — eight shillings taken at a fish market and Pringle's four marks on the top of them, which is what Chapter 92's text already said and what its second column was reaching for. The twenty-eighth is **six marks in**, counted out of the clerk's coat on the step in front of three people with the hour written and read back twice, because **the office paid the air and the pump and the money is the yard's income, not the yard's money, and a book of checkable figures cannot leave it in the air.** **The bag therefore reads 3, 3, 0, 0, 8, 7, 7, 13, 13, 13, 13, 13, 13** and closes at **thirteen in a bag** on the third of May, and the note closes at twenty-three and the tin is empty. The **bag stands at nothing for five days, from the afternoon of the twentieth to the evening of the twenty-fifth**, and it is the three marks of bread on the twentieth that empties it. **Chapter 96's stray *five marks that came out on Wednesday the twenty-second* is removed**: the bag was at nothing on the twenty-second, no chapter books an outgo on it, and a wage week does not fall due on a Wednesday, so the sentence could not be true in any reading. The sixteen marks of wage stand as one payment on the twenty-ninth. **Five chapter figures move with this and nothing else does: Chapters 95, 96, 98, 99 and 100 all read the bag aloud.** The 3, 0, 8, 7, 7, 7, 7, 7, 7, 7 chain this section used to carry was itself wrong and is withdrawn.
2. **Twelve weekday errors on load-bearing dates, nine of them the reviewer's and three found while applying the repair.** The **twenty-ninth of April is a Saturday** and was called a Friday, a Thursday and a Saturday in three chapters; the **thirtieth is a Sunday** and was called *Friday the thirtieth*; **Pringle said the cause in a market on Tuesday the twenty-fifth and not on the Saturday**, and her own later *I said the cause on Saturday* is now the Tuesday; **the second lay's fire is Monday the twenty-fourth** and was called a Friday in four chapters, three of them inside the glass storm's own account of the twenty-sixth; **the going out is Tuesday the second of May and Sarn's *which is a Monday* is now the Tuesday**; **the iron was offered on Tuesday the second of May and not on the Monday**; and **the clerk's *the wall is wiped tomorrow at eight*, said on the Saturday the twenty-ninth, is now *wiped on Monday at eight*,** which is the morning Chapter 97 writes on it. **The three the review did not raise:** Chapter 92's pump was refused on **Saturday the twenty-second and not Thursday the twenty-second**; Chapter 97 dates the claim given back in the market to **Friday the seventh of April and not Thursday**; and Chapter 98 twice calls the twenty-seventh of February a **Tuesday, when every earlier record in this file and in the other three state files has it as Monday** — the going out, the eleventh logged occasion, the day the bellwright's rule was entered under, and the day the plug came out of the bell are all one Monday. **Every date in Chapters 91–100 now matches lock L1's grid, and it has been checked mechanically, name by name, against that grid.** § 1 above is corrected to the same grid and its weekday column is gone, and it no longer claims a **twenty-first of April** that no chapter uses. **The twenty-fifth's earlier chapter also had Nessa Pike's run to the chandler's shop on a *Thursday night* and a *Thursday* before the fire; the fire is the twenty-fourth, so it is now *last night* and *at eight this morning*,** which is the only reading in which she can have heard about a coal store on the strength of one.
3. **The delay measurement carried two dates.** Chapter 99's third column put the boy's own figure *at ten minutes past two on Monday the first of May*; **the bell, the figure and the flat calm are all at about two o'clock on Tuesday the second of May**, in the same chapter, in the same hour, on the same water the boy sounded with his own hand lead. **The column now says the second of May, and so does its own list of the other four days the figure has been taken, and the third column's head — read on the mole's steps at ten past three on the second of May and written in the book at half past five — is on the same day as the thing it records.** The wrong date had been copied into this file, into `state/current.md` and into the queued close prompt, which instructed the audit to re-derive the calendar from the chapters while stating the wrong date in advance; **all three are corrected and the close prompt no longer states it.**
4. **Bevin Rook was twenty-one in one line of Chapter 93 and twenty-two in the other, and in twelve other places.** He is **twenty-two** — the line is corrected, and § 4 above already had him right.
5. **The rescue depth was given in fathoms and in feet.** Five fathoms is the water at the Accountants and eleven feet is not the same water. **The man in the water is now in five fathoms everywhere**: Chapter 95's search, his second column, the line about the hose — which is **ten** feet of hose in Chapter 94, Chapter 97 and the state files, and not eleven — and Chapter 100's read-out. **The bell's eight and a half feet is a pole measurement and stays in feet, and the cairn's eleven feet on the road is a different eleven feet and stays where it is.** Chapter 95's sweep *in about four feet of water on the face of the shoal* is now four fathoms, so that the shoal is in one unit.
6. **Kest's hearing record misdescribed the man in the water in the one entry meant to be read in Veyr.** It called him *a man of nineteen of the third lay with a shackle of this office's own mooring in his hand* and made him **one of the five who went in**. He is **Ivo Trethewey, thirty-one, of the second lay, at the top of the make since the twenty-ninth of March, and he was found and not one of the five**, and the shackle is of the ninth seam's own mooring and not of the authority's. **The record now says so, and says that this office enters him as a man found and not as a man worked, and the wrong sentence is gone.** Two lines in Chapter 95 that called the man in the water *the boy* — where the boy of twenty-two was in the boat with a slate — are **the man**, including the read-back's first column, where the identification of Ivo Trethewey is the point of the line. **The wall's chalk figure is corrected with it, from *FIVE IN THE WATER, FRIDAY. NONE OF THEM OF THIS HARBOR* to *FIVE OFF THIS MOLE, FRIDAY. NONE OF THEM ON THIS OFFICE***, which is what the five men of Chapter 94 actually were and what the record goes on to say: two hired of a working boat and this office's tide gang, this office's foreman as a tender, and two of a yard that has not been on this office's work since the thirty-first of October. **Neither half of that figure was supportable as written and both are now checkable from the mole.**
7. **Chapter 91's wage column contradicted itself.** *Seven lines deep* in the fifth line and *eight lines in the wage column and thirty-two marks* in the seventh, for the same Monday. **Seven lines and twenty-eight marks is the Monday the twenty-fourth**, which is the eleventh week and the fifth of March the oldest of the seven, and **eight lines and thirty-two marks is Tuesday the twenty-fifth**, which Chapter 92 gives. The read-out now carries both and says which day each belongs to.
8. **The wage payments were misstated in three state files.** They said the ninth, tenth and eleventh were paid and the twelfth and three arrears unpaid. **Chapters 96, 98, 99 and 100 all say the ninth, tenth, eleventh and twelfth were paid — four lines, sixteen marks — and that the fifth to the eighth remain.** This file, `state/current.md` and `state/open-threads.md` are all corrected to the chapters.
9. **The Pringle pronoun defect was misdescribed everywhere it was recorded, and the audit was being told to rule and repair on the wrong facts.** The wrong pronouns are in **Chapter 62, line 71** — *a skipper called Pringle who has not paid me for it since November and knows exactly what he is doing* — and in **Chapter 69, lines 29 and 71** — *from Edric Pringle, on his own ring and his own warp and his own account* and *for one day's work on his own ring and his own warp and his own account*. **Seven occurrences across Chapters 62 and 69.** Not Chapter 63, which says *a herring skipper called Edric Pringle* and no pronoun at all, and not *twice*. **Every state file and the queued close prompt now carry the true count, the true chapters and the true line references, so that the audit rules on the defect as it is.**
10. **"The hearing room is used once" was false and was asserted in four files.** Chapter 94 puts the clerk in the hearing room over the tide-gang store at half past nine on **Thursday the twenty-seventh**, reading the yard's reason into the office's own ledger — the office's own record room and not a sitting — **and Chapter 94 called the wall behind the reeve's bench *bare and wiped at eight* on a Thursday, when the wipe is a Monday act. It now says the wall is bare as it has stood since the fifteenth of March, it being wiped every Monday at eight whatever is or is not on it**, which is what a municipal room is and what the wall's own six weeks of emptiness says. **The corrected claim, now in four files, is: the hearing room is used twice in this batch, once for the office's own ledger on Thursday the twenty-seventh and once for the one sitting on Monday the first of May, and the market wall is used once on Wednesday the third of May, and no two of the three are the same day.**
11. **"No chapter is two rooms" was asserted and contradicted.** This file conceded in one breath that Chapters 97 and 98 are both the first of May and are two rooms on one day, and the other three files claimed the opposite. **The true position is now recorded in four files and it is not clean: Chapter 98 is one day in three rooms — the net loft, the mole's steps and the shed — and Chapter 92 is one day in two, the shed and the market boards, and Chapter 100 is one day in two, the boards and the dock.** Every one of the six chapters past the guideline is one complete day or one complete sequence, and no cost is paid twice, and **the shape instruction is carried to Volume 03 with the three exceptions named rather than with a claim that cannot be made.**
12. **Chapter 99's read-back count collapsed.** *The five of them read it back, and the sixth man was the clerk and the seventh was Harl Dace … and the fourth was the holder* put Dace inside the five and the holder at the fourth of the same five. **It now names the five — the boy, the yard, the shipwright, and the two who were in the boat, Sarn and Pike — and the sixth is the clerk and the seventh is Dace, neither of them asked, and the holder read it after his own crew for the second time in two days**, which is the count Chapter 98 gives and the count the holder's three read-backs in two days require.
13. **Coll Ferrow acquired a defect that is not his.** Chapter 99 gave *a man of thirty-one with a bad wheel and nine days in this harbor because of it*, and **Chapter 89 gave the same defect and the same nine days to a boat** — the *Kestrel's Sister* of the second lay, of which Coll Ferrow is the man. **The bad wheel is the boat's and Chapter 99 now says so, and § 5 above names Ferrow as the man of her.** The man of fifty-one of the *Grey Widow* keeps a bad wheel **of his own**; **the two wheels are two wheels in two boats and in two men, they are not one defect, and no chapter may fuse them.**

### Prose, precision and mechanical repairs

14. **Chapter 100 called Edric Pringle *she* in *and she came down the boards in her oilskins*** — she is a woman and the noun is **her oilskin**. Fixed.
15. **Chapter 92's slate read *has beenwet to the waist*** — missing space. Fixed.
16. **Chapter 100 had *a cairn and a creek and a bar of gravel and a man in a panel, and and a man in a market*** — duplicated word. Fixed.
17. **Chapter 94 gave the writ's fee three different figures in three consecutive paragraphs** — *one shilling and sixpence*, then *a mark and sixpence*, then *a mark of the yard's*. **The fee is one shilling and sixpence throughout**, as in Chapter 97, Chapter 98 and every state file, and Nessa Pike's sentence is now about the shilling and sixpence she actually paid.
18. **Chapter 92's tray did not add up to its own total, and Pringle named the sum ambiguously.** The tray held one shilling, two shillings, a shilling between two women and two lots of threepence, which is four shillings and sixpence, and the chapter said the tray held eight shillings; and Pringle's *the number is eight shillings and four* could be read as a total against a tray count *plus* her own four marks, in a book whose engine is checkable figures. **The tray now holds eight shillings — one, two, one between two women, three more men at a shilling each, and four lots of threepence, which is a shilling — and the arithmetic is on the page in Pringle's own mouth, which is the one place in this volume where a woman insists on being exact. Her line is now *eight shillings out of that tray and my four marks on the top of it*, the second column carries the same split, and Chapter 92's last line says which is which.** No figure moved but the eight.
19. **Three characters said the same sentence about fifty-one years in this harbor.** Tristram's stays, because he is the wharfinger and the elder. **Dace's man of fifty-one now says forty-nine**, which is his own age's neighbour and not a number any other mouth in the batch uses, and **Edric Pringle no longer says a number at all** — *I have been in this harbor since I was eleven years old* — because hers is the sentence about a cause of hers being put right way up, and it does not need a figure to be the loudest thing on a market wall.
20. **The bell's second column was stated three times in near-identical wording across two days** — Chapter 98's spoken account, Chapter 98's record of it, and Chapter 99's column for the going out. The first two are the volume's own device, a man speaking and the book entering what he said, and they stand. **Chapter 99's two columns now enter what was entered on Monday by reference and add only what is new** — *the fouling, the mass and the ground line, being the same as they were entered on Monday out of the same mouth, and about the same still not being a figure* — because a book that re-enters the same figures at the same length is not a book anybody can check.
21. **Chapter 98 said the holder had watched the boy's hand go for twenty-eight days** on the first of May, and the hand has been going since the fourth of April. **Twenty-seven days.** Fixed.
22. **Chapter 94 dated the pot's purchase to a Thursday of the following week that nothing happens on.** It is now *a day was coming that would want a pot filled*, which is the reason the chapter gives for buying air in advance and does not need a date.
23. **Chapter 99's sixth-hand passage carried two dates that were not days.** *It took him all of the third of May* and *brought it back on the Thursday of the third of May* — the copies went out on the Tuesday the second, and the third of May is a Wednesday. Ferrow's copy is now *the whole of that day and most of the night*, and Trask's is on the bench at ten on the Wednesday morning of the third, which is the morning Chapter 100 says one of the six is on a table in the south lay.
24. **Chapter 96 carried a boat's name that is not a boat's name** — *the ground where the *Hard I suppose* was lying*. It is now *an old boat of the third lay*, which is what the sentence needs, and no new vessel is named.
25. **The state files and the queued close prompt pre-stated three rulings the chapters contradict** — the delay's date, the Pringle chapters and count, and the hearing-room count — while instructing the audit to re-derive all three from the chapters. **The close prompt now states none of them and tells the audit to rule each from the chapters**, which is what it says it is doing.

### One unflagged defect of the same family was found while applying the rest, and is repaired here

**Chapter 99 names five hands where the wall says six.** Sella Brigg, Harl Dace, Edric Pringle, Coll Ferrow and Oswin Trask are named and described in the chapter; the wall, the tenth thing, the boy's refusal to be counted as a writer and the chapter's own title all say **six hands and a mouth**, and Chapter 100 puts a man of the second lay at the head of the boards asking for a seventh copy. **No sixth writer is invented here.** The count on the wall stands at six, five of the six are named on the page with the hand described, **the sixth is a man in this harbor who copied a sheet and is not named, and the volume audit is the phase that may say who he is or rule that he stays unnamed.** What the repair fixes is the state file, which recorded six hands and named five with no gap acknowledged.

### Verified in the repair and not changed

**The money chain reconciles mark by mark and the bag now closes at thirteen on the third of May** (3, 3, 0, 0, 8, 7, 7, 13, 13, 13, 13, 13, 13), the note at **30, 30, 33, 33, 33, 33, 33, 33, 27, 23**, and the tin **2 crowns, 8s 6d, empty**. The three counts of the note's weeks are still twenty-two, thirteen and eleven and none of them moved. The eleven copies, the four unreached holders, the eleven logged occasions and the two empty answers are untouched, and **the count of occasions was not raised and the delay figure is still a figure and not an occasion.** The eleven feet of the cairn, the eight and a half feet of the bell, the five fathoms of the Accountants, the two and three-quarters of the bar and the seventy fathoms west-southwest are all in their own units and none of them moved. The four names on the writ stand, the reporting clause stands with three names on paper and no face in the room, the objection is still unsummarized, and Mara Venn still says before she is asked that the writ cannot reach her. The claim window is still worked, logged and raised nothing, and **no salvage award was ever claimable and the volume does not pretend otherwise.** The glass storm is in the second column with a reason against it and no cause named. The lead plug is in a chest in a shed with four descriptions that do not agree, a number nobody will write down, and no connection to the plate dug in February. The bell is not an instrument and the Bellwright stage is not taken. No power stage is taken, the condition is not named anywhere in Chapters 91–100, Alden Marr confesses nothing and Calder Marr does not go. No custody object returns, the compartment is not referred to, the nine-line mark is not explained, and the word *network* is used once by a rival and refused in public. **The volume's resolution is intact and it is not waived: eight marks to the bellwright paid out of four hands in a market, sixteen marks of a boy's wage out of four hands in a market, the yard's own debt to Mara Venn not paid and not reachable by the writ, a Kest payment that cost a woman four hundred feet of her own warp and a berth, and the chandler's note not re-written and said out loud on a wall.** **No interface vocabulary, no panel, no stat block, no second person in narration, no duplicated paragraph and no exact repeated sentence anywhere in the batch.** The corrected word counts are **33,327** (Ch91 2,791 · Ch92 2,529 · Ch93 2,972 · Ch94 3,211 · Ch95 3,931 · Ch96 3,435 · Ch97 3,978 · Ch98 3,524 · Ch99 3,136 · Ch100 3,820), measured with `wc -w` including headings.
